From Kaira the Acting Collector, Mr. Elliot, wrote on 30th September—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"1. In reply to your Circular dated 1st July 1867, I have the honor to lay before you such scanty accounts of famines in Goozerat as I have been able to obtain, principally from tradition and memory of old inhabitants, there being no records available.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"2. The famine which occurred in the year <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1791-92 was by far the most severe of any of which anything is known. Very little rain fell during the year. There was, it is said, only one fall, which occurred in July or August, and a scarcity followed which obliged many of the inhabitants of this part of Goozerat to go into Malwa, where they suffered terribly from cholera, or some other epidemic disease. This was in the time of Bappoo Pandra, an Officer of the Guicowar's Government, who seems to have done what he could to help the people by repressive measures against crime, and by remitting the whole of the Revenue collections, except that he took a fourth share of the grass. The annexed list, the figures in which are taken from old mercantile accounts of the day, will shew the prices at which provisions were sold during this year.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"3. In <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1813-14 there was drought, and a corresponding scarcity of food; rain fell in July, and for a month and a half afterwards there was no rain, and then not much. The consequence was great injury to crops, and as the people in Kattyawar and to the west of this were starving, they came to these parts in great numbers with mendicants and others, many of whom died. This was when Mr. Rowl was Collector of Kaira, but I am unable to find any written records of the scarcity, which was followed the next year by cholera. I append a list of prices at which grain, &amp;c., was selling.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"4. In the beginning of the rains of <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1825-26 there was a good fall, and the crops were doing well, but subsequently the rain held off, and in consequence the crops were a good deal below the average, and a remission of Rs. 1,61,987-6-0 in the revenue had to be made, but this was clearly nothing more than a bad season, and I do not find that there was any starvation.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"5. In <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1834-35 a great loss of cattle occurred, caused by locusts, which ate up the grass and crops; but in this year nothing like a famine seems to have occurred, but 1,96,554 Rupees of revenue was remitted.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"6. I regret to have to tell you that these very scanty materials are all that I have to offer you for the purposes of your report.</p></blockquote></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-63"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="63" data-folio="51" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n62_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 51">[p. 51]</button></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-014"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-014 · FOLIO 63</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/report-past-famines-bombay-etheridge-1868/datasets/tables/tbl-014_p0063.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/report-past-famines-bombay-etheridge-1868/datasets/tidy/tbl-014_p0063.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><caption><em>List shewing the prices at which pro- visions were sold during the year 1791-92 for one Rupee.</em> | <em>List of prices at which grain, &amp;c., were sold during the year 1813-14 for one Rupee.</em></caption><thead><tr><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col">Seers.</th><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col">Seers.</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>Bajree</td><td class="num">18</td><td>Bajree</td><td class="num">17¼</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Wheat</td><td class="num">16</td><td>Wheat</td><td class="num">10</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Kodra</td><td class="num">27</td><td>Ghee</td><td class="num">3½</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Joowar</td><td class="num">12½</td><td>Jogree</td><td class="num">9½</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Gram</td><td class="num">16</td><td>Cumin Seed</td><td class="num">6</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Toor</td><td class="num">20</td><td>Joowar</td><td class="num">17</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Rice in husk</td><td class="num">24</td><td>Toor</td><td class="num">22½</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Buntee</td><td class="num">26</td><td>Teel</td><td class="num">16</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Ghee</td><td class="num">2½</td><td>Barley</td><td class="num">19</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Chinna</td><td class="num">26</td><td>Kodra</td><td class="num">32</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Goowar</td><td class="num">22</td><td>Rice in husk</td><td class="num">20</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Sweet Oil</td><td class="num">26</td><td>Goowar</td><td class="num">20</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Jogree</td><td class="num">7</td><td>Tobacco</td><td class="num">10</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Teel</td><td class="num">16</td><td>Bowta</td><td class="num">21</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Tobacco</td><td class="num">12</td><td>Gram</td><td class="num">20</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Cumin Seed</td><td class="num">8</td><td>Buntee</td><td class="num">35</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Barley</td><td class="num">22½</td><td>Sweet Oil</td><td class="num">5¼</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Bowta</td><td class="num">22½</td><td>Sugar</td><td class="num">4</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Castor Oil</td><td class="num">7</td><td>Mutt</td><td class="num">18¼</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td></td><td></td><td>Toor Dall</td><td class="num">16</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td></td><td></td><td>Mug</td><td class="num">17</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td></td><td></td><td>Castor Oil</td><td class="num">5¾</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">32. Scanty as the above information may appear, materials did not exist in the Kaira Collectorate for dealing with the subject more fully.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">33. From Baroda the Acting Assistant Resident in charge,- Lieutenant Salmon, wrote on 15th August—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"1. In compliance with your letter dated 1st July last, I have the honor to inform you that there were famines in the Baroda territories in the years <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1791 and 1813, but as to their extent and the causes from which they arose the Durbar are unable to inform me, as there are no records concerning them. During the time they lasted money was freely distributed from the Government Treasury, and labour in the shape of building, digging tanks, and other public works, was provided, by which means the suffering that must otherwise have been inevitable were to a great extent averted.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"2. Since <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1813 no famines whatever have taken place."</p></blockquote></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-64"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="64" data-folio="52" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n63_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 52">[p. 52]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">34. As it appeared to me that neither the Local nor Supreme Government would be satisfied in respect to a subject to which they attached so much interest with the meagre detail furnished, - and that from so important a State as Baroda, I begged the favor of the matter being allowed to lie over till the return of the Resident. After some correspondence, Colonel Barr finally reported, on 27th January last, that every measure had been adopted from the Residency to procure the information required regarding past famines within the Baroda territory, consistent with his position as Resident at that Court and suggested by his political experience, and that the Baroda Durbar, he was fully persuaded, had been anxious to aid his endeavours in furtherance of the wishes of the Supreme Government on the subject; but that, as he had already stated, he was sorry to have to report that authentic details were not forthcoming.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">35. From the Rewa Kanta Province the Political Agent, Captain Barton, wrote as follows on 29th August:—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"1. With reference to your circular letter of the 1st ultimo, I have the honor to enclose a precis of the information I have been able to obtain regarding past famines in the districts under my Political supervision.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"2. The control over the Native States comprised in the Rewa Kanta Division became vested in the British Government in the year <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1825. The only famine that has occurred since that period was in <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1833-34.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"3. I regret that I have been unable to procure more precise information than that which I have submitted. The various Durbars possess no historical records of any description. It is only since the introduction of British supervision that they have begun to emerge from a state of anarchy, internecine strife, and barbarism; and the only records of former events are contained in the oral traditions of village elders.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"4. I have sought to obtain information on the subject referred to from the hereditary Bards of the district. I have not, however, been able to discover any written record of such events. The Bards appear to have confined themselves strictly to entries of family events, and to have neglected such as bore upon the general welfare of the country.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"5. As I have mentioned in the precis, no general distress from failure of water would bear so hard upon the wild inhabitants of the Rewa Kanta as upon those living in settled districts. A Bheel is restless and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-65" data-page="65" data-folio="53" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n64_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 53">[p. 53]</button> migratory by nature. He is accustomed to remove with all his goods and cattle at a moment's notice. He can subsist on roots, on Mhowra berries, &amp;c., and can live well where others would starve; above all, he is essentially predatory, and a marauding party will often find a stray buffaloe, kill, cook, and eat it on the spot, and destroy all traces of its remains. So long as a Bheel can find a pool of water, he is sure to be able to supply himself with food of some sort or another, and in no year, even of severe dearth, have the Nurbudda and Mhye been entirely dried up."</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">36. The precis referred to is thus given—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"1. The tract of country known as the Rewa Kanta is situated between 21° 30′, and 23° 30′, North latitude, and 73° 10′, and 74° 20′, East longitude. It is watered by the Mhye and Nurbudda Rivers and their tributaries, the principal of which are the Pahmunkurd, Goma, and Maysree, flowing into the Mhye, and the Oresing, Erun, and Kurjun, which empty themselves into the Nurbudda. The country is for the most part covered with hills and jungle, the majority of the inhabitants consists of Bheels and Coolees of various denominations. The former are migratory in their habits, and seldom live in settled habitations. There are no large cities, and no means of inter-communication beyond fair weather roads.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"2. The territorial possessions of 62 Chiefs are comprised in the Rewa Kanta Political Division; of these the six principal are Rajpeepla, Oodeypoor, Barea, Soonth, Loonavara, and Ballasinore.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"3. Owing to the nature of the country and its inhabitants, and from the fact of its being watered by two large rivers and their numerous affluents, the Rewa Kanta districts have never been entirely devastated by famine. Still severe distress has sometimes prevailed, and the following are the most notable and well remembered instances.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"4. The first famine of which I can gain any information occurred in the year <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1746-47. Hardly any rain fell in this year, and the crops did not ripen. Many people died, but the wild inhabitants kept themselves and their cattle alive by eating forest roots and locusts, which abounded at the time.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"5. The next year of scarcity occurred in <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1790-91, and was also occasioned by the failure of the periodical rains. This appears to have been the nearest approach to a general famine on record. Poor people sold their children. The Bheels subsisted on jungle roots and berries. Many people and cattle died, and the country was infested with bands of marauders.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"6. The various Durbars appear to have doled out daily food to the crowds that flocked to them, but no general measures of relief were originated. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-66" data-page="66" data-folio="54" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n65_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 54">[p. 54]</button> "7. The next year of scarcity on record was <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1802-3: this also was owing to the want of rain, but the distress was not so great, nor the dearth of crops so general as on the previous occasion.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"8. The year <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1812-13 was one of severe distress, and was attended with the same disasters as that in <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1790-91. Grain was sold at the following prices:—</p></blockquote></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-015"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-015 · FOLIO 66</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/report-past-famines-bombay-etheridge-1868/datasets/tables/tbl-015_p0066.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/report-past-famines-bombay-etheridge-1868/datasets/tidy/tbl-015_p0066.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>Bajree</td><td class="num">8</td><td>Seers for the Rupee.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Toor Dal</td><td class="num">6</td><td>"</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Rice</td><td class="num">6</td><td>"</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Muth</td><td class="num">13</td><td>"</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Moong</td><td class="num">6</td><td>"</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"9. Food was distributed to those who came for assistance by the various Durbars. There also appears to have been some scarcity in the year <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1824-25, but not to any great extent.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"10. The year <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1833-34 was one of general scarcity. A description of the state of affairs will be found in a foot note in pages 201-2 .. of Ras Malla, Book 3. The distress, however, was not nearly so severe as in <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1790-91, or <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1812-13. Some grain was left in the country from the previous years' crops, the people were assisted by the various Durbars, and not many deaths, either of men or cattle, are recorded. In this year a sum of Rs. 52,952-5-3 was remitted from the whole tribute of Rs. 1,70,394-4-0 by order of the Government.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"11. There have been no famines since the last mentioned year: there was a scanty rain-fall in the year <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1838-39, when a sum of Rs. 27,903-6-0 was remitted from the tribute. Again, in the year <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1848-49 remissions were granted to the extent of Rs. 4,269.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"12. Provisions were very dear in the year <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1864-65, grain being sold at 8 seers for the Rupee. This, however, was owing to extraneous causes (the American war, and consequent rise in cotton), and not to want of rain."</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">37. From the Sub-Collectorate of Broach, Mr. Hogg forwarded reports from the Deputy Collector and Third Assistant in October, as follows:—</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>From the Deputy Collector.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"1. With reference to your letter No. 1339, dated the 26th July last, requesting me to submit certain information on the subject of past famines in this country, I have the honor to report that I have been unable to obtain any authentic information on the subject, either from Government <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-67" data-page="67" data-folio="55" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n66_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 55">[p. 55]</button> records or from hereditary Officers; but on making inquires from old persons in this town, I learn that famines occurred in this country in the years Sumvut 1847, 1869 and 1891, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1790-91, 1812-13, and 1834-35.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“2. The famine in Sumvut 1847, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1790-91, is said to be caused by scarcity of rain; while in the provinces of Kattyawar and Marwar there was no rain at all, by which numbers of persons from those provinces were driven by necessity to this country, and consequently the prices of grain, &amp;c., were raised very high, as follows:—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-016"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-016 · FOLIO 67</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/report-past-famines-bombay-etheridge-1868/datasets/tables/tbl-016_p0067.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/report-past-famines-bombay-etheridge-1868/datasets/tidy/tbl-016_p0067.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>Wheat</td><td colspan="2">Rs. 60 per Kulsee of 16 maunds.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Joowar</td><td>Rs. 35 per Do.</td><td>do.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Bajree</td><td>Rs. 45 per Do.</td><td>do.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Ghee</td><td colspan="2">Rs. 14 per maund.</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“The famine in this year was all over Goozerat, and lasted, it is said, for one year. To alleviate the distress, Muzmoodar Lulloobhoy and Assaram, who had large quantities of grain stored in their warehouses out of previous years’ crops, distributed the same to poor persons in charity, and those who were possessed of money purchased it for their maintenance; certain rich persons commenced to build very large houses in this year, which procured employment to many poor persons, who were provided with grain in return for their labour.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“3. The next famine occurred in Sumvut 1869, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1812-13, not by the failure of rain, but by the crops before they were reaped being entirely devoured by locusts, which came in very large numbers, and spread over all the country. In this year, too, numbers of persons came to this country from Kattyawar and Marwar, which raised the prices of grain very high. The famine, it is said, extended to the district of Surat Athaveesee, Broach, Kattyawar, Marwar, and all over Goozerat, and lasted for one year. It is not known what measures for relief were adopted, but it is said that the local Officers had issued strict orders to the Bunias and others who had large quantities of grain stored by them out of previous years’ crops, to sell no more grain to people than what was actually required to maintain life, and the sales were superintended by Government Officers.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“4. The famine which occurred in Sumvut 1891, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1835, was caused by excessive rain, which destroyed crops in the fields, and the subsequent winter crops were burnt down by intense cold. This famine extended all over Goozerat, including Surat Athaveesee. The prices were as follows:—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-017"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-017 · FOLIO 67</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/report-past-famines-bombay-etheridge-1868/datasets/tables/tbl-017_p0067.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/report-past-famines-bombay-etheridge-1868/datasets/tidy/tbl-017_p0067.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>“ Wheat</td><td colspan="2">Rs. 68 per Kulsee of 16 maunds.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>“ Joowar</td><td>Rs. 27 per do.</td><td>do.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>“ Bajree</td><td>Rs. 27 per do.</td><td>do.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>“ Ghee</td><td colspan="2">Rs. 22 per one maund.</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-68"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="68" data-folio="56" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n67_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 56">[p. 56]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">To alleviate the distress Government granted large remissions to cultivators and advanced Tuccavee, and until crops were produced in the following year the people managed to maintain themselves in the best way they could.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“5. The reports from the Mumladars of Wagra and Broach on the subject are herewith submitted, as requested. They are meagre, and afford no valuable information. The Mumladar of Broach reports that famine occurred in the years <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1630 and 1681 previous to the establishment of the British rule in this country, when the Mahomedan rulers distributed grain in charity and granted large remission to cultivators.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">“<em>From the Third Assistant, Mr. McKenzie.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“1. In reply to your Nos. 1338 and 1476 of 1867, dated respectively 26th July and 19th August 1867, I have the honor to forward for your information the following facts, which I have elicited by personal enquiry and research as to the extent and number of famines in this district.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“2. In Sumvut 1847, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1791, the famine throughout the Broach Zillah was most severe and destructive, so much so that great numbers of people perished, and the year has since been remembered as ‘the famine year’ par excellence, and forms an era from which events take their date.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“3. Twenty-two years afterwards another famine raged throughout the north and centre of Goozerat, but it does not seem to have touched Broach itself so severely, as of the actual and permanent cultivators and inhabitants comparatively few died. The were many deaths from famine it is true, but these occurred chiefly among the emigrants from other districts.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“4. The last famine occurred <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1819-20, when owing to two extraordinarily heavy monsoons the population was severely distressed, though apparently few or none died, the fact being that they were better able to cope with distress than in previous years. Owing to the extreme difficulty of getting information by personal enquiry at this time of the year, I am unable to give you any further information.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“5. I also enclose a table of the highest reported prices of grain in Broach from <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1810 to <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1820.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-69"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="69" data-folio="57" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n68_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 57">[p. 57]</button></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-018"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-018 · FOLIO 69</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/report-past-famines-bombay-etheridge-1868/datasets/tables/tbl-018_p0069.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/report-past-famines-bombay-etheridge-1868/datasets/tidy/tbl-018_p0069.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><caption><span class="sc">Reported Highest Prices of Grain in Broach from A. D.</span> 1810 <span class="sc">to</span> 1820. <em>Per Kulsee of 16 Maunds, or 640 Seers.</em></caption><thead><tr><th scope="col">Years.</th><th scope="col">Years.</th><th scope="col">Joowar.</th><th scope="col">Wheat.</th><th scope="col">Dungur.</th><th scope="col">Bajree.</th><th scope="col">Kodra.</th><th scope="col">Baota.</th><th scope="col">Mutt.</th><th scope="col">Toor.</th></tr><tr><th scope="col">Rs. a. p.</th><th scope="col">Rs. a. p.</th><th scope="col">Rs. a. p.</th><th scope="col">Rs. a. p.</th><th scope="col">Rs. a. p.</th><th scope="col">Rs. a. p.</th><th scope="col">Rs. a. p.</th><th scope="col">Rs. a. p.</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">1866</td><td class="num">1810</td><td class="num">9 0 0</td><td class="num">1871</td><td class="num">1815</td><td class="num">15 4 0</td><td class="num">18 0 0</td><td class="num">14 0 0</td><td class="num">18 4 0</td><td class="num">9 4 0</td><td class="num">13 4 0</td><td class="num">14 8 0</td><td class="num">14 0 0</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">1872</td><td class="num">1816</td><td class="num">13 0 0</td><td class="num">18 0 0</td><td class="num">1873</td><td class="num">1817</td><td class="num">16 0 0</td><td class="num">17 0 0</td><td class="num">13 8 0</td><td class="num">16 0 0</td><td class="num">10 0 0</td><td class="num">11 class="num">1875</td><td class="num">1819</td><td class="num">18 0 0</td><td class="num">18 0 0</td><td class="num">14 4 0</td><td class="num">16 0 0</td><td class="num">13 8 0</td><td class="num">12 0 0</td><td class="num">13 0 0</td><td class="num">14 0 0</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">1876</td><td class="num">1820</td><td class="num">28 0 0</td><td class="num">39 0 0</td><td class="num">19 0 0</td><td class="num">39 0 0</td><td class="num">18 0 0</td><td class="num">26 0 0</td><td class="num">22 0 0</td><td class="num">18 0 0</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-70"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="70" data-folio="58" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n69_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 58">[p. 58]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">38. Mr. Hogg, the Sub-Collector of Broach, upon a further reference, declared his inability to supply any more information.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">39. From Surat the Collector, Mr. Hope, forwarded, on 16th October last, the following reports from his Assistants and Deputies:—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“<em>From the First Assistant, Mr. Spry, dated 19th August.</em></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“1. In accordance with your Memorandum No. 1867, dated the 10th July last, I have the honor to furnish such information as I can obtain respecting past years of famine and scarcity in the districts under my charge. I much regret that the time of year has prevented my making personal enquiries in the districts, from which doubtless I might have obtained many interesting facts from intelligent Natives; as it is, I am mainly dependent on details obtained from the different Mumladars.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“2. The first famine of which there is any records appears to have taken place in Sumvut 1803, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1746, over 120 years ago. There is, however, but little or nothing reliable known of it beyond the fact; and indeed mention is made of it by only one Mumladar out of four, whose reports I now have before me.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“3. The next famine took place in Sumvut 1847, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1790. This famine appears to have arisen from a general failure of the monsoon rains, and consequent failure of the harvest. It seems to have been very widely spread, and I conclude must have been far severer in the Deccan, Concan, and other provinces, than in Goozerat, as numbers of the inhabitants of those districts fled for subsistence into the Surat districts, and the mortality from starvation appears to have been much greater amongst the fugitive than amongst the fixed population. Part of the districts under report was then under the rule of the Peishwa and part under the Guicowar. General remission of tribute was, it seems, granted by both Governments; and further to alleviate the distress the Bazar rates were fixed. The Peishwa, moreover, not only forbade the exportation of grain, but imported it from Bombay and other places.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“4. The third famine occurred 13 years after the one above alluded to, namely, in Sumvut 1860, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1803-4, and appears to have been very similar to it, and, like it, to have caused a great influx of starving poor from the adjacent provinces, and the mortality appears to have been confined to them alone. During this year, although there was without doubt a scarcity of rain and great dearness in the price of food in these districts, I am inclined to believe that the great distress was mainly caused by the sudden increase of population, rather than by local causes. Remission of revenue was <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-71" data-page="71" data-folio="59" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n70_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 59">[p. 59]</button> granted by the then existing Governments, who also fixed the Bazar prices and forbade exportation, and in Bugwara it seems that labourers were employed and paid in grain. Much, moreover, seems to have been done by private charity. Rambhai Mota, Desai of Chicklee, is reported to have given away large quatities of grain to the poor, and one Nagurdass Lallkissondass, then Mumlatur of Soopa, is stated to have imported grain into his Talooka, and sold it at reasonable prices.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">" 5. The above, happily, are the only ones which can strictly be termed famine years, although we have since then had seasons of more or less dearth. Amongst the most prominent of these was Sumvut 1881, <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1824-5, when very general remissions were granted, and Government gave one lac of Rupees in this Zillah for expenditure in making tanks, wells, &amp;c. Sumvut 1895, <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1838-9, also was a year of unusual scarcity, when considerable remissions were given, and the poor employed on public works.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">" 6. Other years of comparative scarcity which have now and again occurred have nothing noticeable in them, as I cannot find that the permanent condition of the country or population was affected by them. I, therefore, refrain from entering into details concerning them.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>" From the Deputy Collector, dated 6th September.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">" 1. In reply to your endorsement No. 1109, under date the 10th July last, on printed copy of Circular dated 1st idem from the Compiler Presidency Famine Report, I have the honor to submit the following information respecting past famines in the districts in my charge.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Mandvee.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">" 2. The year Sumvut 1847, <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1790-91, was one of dearth throughout the whole district. There was no rain then : this resulted in a failure of crops, and the death from starvation of many people; a large number of persons were forced to maintain themselves on roots. The disaster, which was a general one, brought in numbers of poor men from abroad to the town of Mandvee, and on that occasion the Raja of Mandvee displayed his liberality by the distribution of grain daily as an alleviation of the misery of the sufferers. The district lapsed to the British Government in <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1848.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Walore.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">" 3. This district was also visited with famine during the year Sumvut 1847, <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1790-91, but not to such an extent as in Mandvee, consequently no one fell a victim to it. The rain was scanty, and the crops were proportionately scarce. In the year Sumvut 1860, <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1803-4, although the fall of rain was limited, the crops flourished. Famine had made its appearance in the Deccan at that time ; consequently people dying with hunger had emigrated into this district, some of whom died of having had nothing to subsist upon. The prices of grain <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-72" data-page="72" data-folio="60" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n71_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 60">[p. 60]</button> had risen then. In the years Sumvut 1881 and 1895, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1824-25 and 1838-39, the fall of rain was also scanty. All other crops but rice were grown, but not so well as could be expected; remission in consequence was granted by Government to the cultivators of land. No deaths had occurred owing to the scarcity of provisions. The British supremacy was established in this Talooka in the month of Jest Sumvut 1859, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1802-3.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Bardolee.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">"4. During the former regime this district had to encounter severe attacks from famine in the years Sumvut 1803 and 1847, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1746-47 and 1790-91. There was no rain then, and the result was a failure of crops and destruction of the lives of many men and cattle. The land tax was wholly remitted to the cultivators, and advances were made to them as the source whereby to procure the necessaries of life, which were then at an exceedingly high price.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">"5. Subsequent to the establishment of the British power the district presented no aspect of famine. The scarcity of rain, however, during the years Sumvut 1859, 1869, 1881, 1895, 1905 and 1910, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1802-3, 1812-13, 1824-25, 1838-39, 1848-49 and 1853-54, and the consequent failure of crops in some places, accelerated the increase in the prices of grain. Famine was prevalent in the Deccan in the year Sumvut 1859, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1802-3; hence the people of the poorer classes migrated into this district, and committed acts of depredation at the hazard of their lives, and to the great detriment of the inhabitants. Government had, however, adopted measures for the security of the lives and properties of their subjects. Tuccavee was advanced to them during the first two years, as an incentive to take up cultivation, and remission was made of the revenue dues during the other years proportionate to the amount of damages sustained."</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>"From the District Deputy Collector, dated 30th August.</em></p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">“<span class="sc">Famine Report</span> of the Pergunnahs of Olpar and Unclesir, including Hansote.</h3><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-019"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-019 · FOLIO 72</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/report-past-famines-bombay-etheridge-1868/datasets/tables/tbl-019_p0072.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/report-past-famines-bombay-etheridge-1868/datasets/tidy/tbl-019_p0072.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><caption>“<span class="sc">Famine Report</span> of the Pergunnahs of Olpar and Unclesir, including Hansote.</caption><thead><tr><th scope="col">Sumvut Year.</th><th scope="col">Corresponding Christian Year.</th><th scope="col">Purgunnahs.</th><th scope="col"></th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">1803</td><td class="num">1747</td><td>Unclesir and Hansote...</td><td>Great famine. Cultivators fled to Malwa for food. Government called them back and made remissions of rent, and also made advances in the shape of Tuccavee. During this year of great distress the then Government distributed grain free of charge; some portions of the Purgunnahs were not-withstanding desolated. The information is obtained from the old hereditary revenue officers. No record, private or public, is forthcoming.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td><button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-73" data-page="73" data-folio="61" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n72_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 61">[p. 61]</button> 1836 &amp; 1837 ...</td><td>1780 &amp; 1781 ...</td><td>Unclesir...................</td><td>Years of dearness, but not of famine. The scarcity was caused by the plunderings of the Pindarees.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">1838.............</td><td class="num">1782 ............</td><td>Do. ....................</td><td>For particulars see Sumvut 1803, Unclesir.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>" ...............</td><td>" ............</td><td>Hansote....................</td><td>Great famine, and much distress; but no detail information is procurable.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">1847.............</td><td class="num">1791 ............</td><td>Olpar .....................</td><td>Great famine. People fled for grain to other countries. Some of the people offered their services for food, while others lived by begging; some of them took refuge at Surat, where the Native Shaookars, and particularly the Dutch Merchants, supplied the people with rice free of charge, or by receiving a reasonable price, according as the people were indigent or otherwise. The then Nawab of Surat also helped the distressed poor to a considerable degree, and made arrangements by which the grain dealers were made to sell according to a certain nerrick a fixed quantity of grain to each individual.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>" ...............</td><td>" ............</td><td>Unclesir ..................</td><td>For particulars vide Sumvut 1803, Unclesir.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>" ...............</td><td>" ............</td><td>Hansote....................</td><td>This was a year of great famine, and scarcity of water and food to men and animals. The then Government procured grain, opened grain shops, and supplied each family with the requisite quantity of grain as far as possible, but the supply not being sufficient, large numbers of men and animals began to die; so the people with their cattle fled to Malwa, where several of them died, and others returned on the return of the monsoon.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">1860.............</td><td class="num">1804 ............</td><td>Olpar .....................</td><td>For particulars see Sumvut 1847, Olpar.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>" ...............</td><td>" ............</td><td>Unclesir...................</td><td>Grain scarce, but not so scarce as to produce great hardship. The cultivators were allowed remission by Government, and advances were also made to them in the shape of Tuccavee; so they had not much difficulty in returning to their former state.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">1869.............</td><td class="num">1813 ............</td><td>Olpar .....................</td><td>For particulars, vide Sumvut 1847, Olpar.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>" ...............</td><td>" ..............</td><td>Hansote....................</td><td>Rain was scanty, but not so scanty as to cause great distress from want of water and grain, so the people had not to leave their native land.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>" ...............</td><td>" ..............</td><td>Unclesir...................</td><td>For particulars, vide Sumvut 1860, Unclesir.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-74" data-page="74" data-folio="62" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n73_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 62">[p. 62]</button> 1881</td><td class="num">1825</td><td>Unclesir</td><td>For particulars, vide Sumvut 1860, Unclesir.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>”</td><td>”</td><td>Hansote</td><td>Do. Sumvut 1869, Hansote.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">1895</td><td class="num">1839</td><td>Unclesir</td><td>Do. do. 1860.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>”</td><td>”</td><td>Hansote</td><td>There was an insufficient fall of rain in general. In some eight or ten seacoast villages there was a complete failure of rain; so the people of these villages left their homes and settled themselves in foreign territories, while others who became indigent received the Tuccavee (advances) from Government, and were allowed reasonable remissions in the Jummabundy settlement, in order to enable them to settle themselves and perform their occupations in the then next rainy season.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">1897</td><td class="num">1841</td><td>Do.</td><td>For particulars, see Sumvut 1869, Hansote.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>”</td><td>”</td><td>Olpar</td><td>No great famine has taken place since the accession of the British Government. In Sumvut 1874, A. D. 1818, however, in some years crops were injured owing to the scarcity of rain, or the overflowing of the river. In such times as this Government allowed remissions of rent to the cultivators, and made donations to enable the people to build houses. Government also found work for the people by opening public works, such as sinking and repairing wells, clearing tanks, &amp;c., so the people had not to fly for grain to other provinces. The price of grain and other necessaries of life between the Sumvut 1816 and 1821 was not better than the famine prices in the years preceding the accession of the British power, and, though the prices have declined since the Sumvut 1822, the decrease is only slight.</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>From the Hoozoor Deputy Collector, dated 30th September.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“1. Conformably to your endorsement No. 1109, of the 10th July last, on a copy of a Circular dated 1st idem from the Compiler of the Presidency Famine Report, I have the honor to submit such information as I could gather on the subject of famines which prevailed in Goozerat on different occasions.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-75"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="75" data-folio="63" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n74_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 63">[p. 63]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">"2. 'Miratey Ahmedee' contains the following account regarding famines on five occasions :—</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">" ' I. Hijree 1038, Samvut 1685-86, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1628-29.</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">" ' This famine is called the ' Satmasia :' it lasted for seven months. No rain having fallen, the people were sadly in want of bread and water, and were compelled to sell their children, for whom purchasers were not to be found. One camel was procurable for one Rupee. The benevolent Nobles could not support the poor. Those who used to support and protect the poor were obliged to beg. The flesh of dogs was eaten like that of sheep and goats, and their bones were ground and mixed with flour, for which the grain dealers were punished. When the people were very much distressed by this famine, they began to kill men, and to use their flesh. If a single person happened to meet three or four persons, he was afraid of his life. The roads were thus deserted, and the loss of life was very great. Men of strength and power proceeded to other countries. The Ahmedabad Zillah was nearly ruined. The Emperor ordered the Chiefs and Nobles at Ahmedabad, Surat, and Burranpoor, to establish Institutions for the distribution of food and water to the sufferers. In Ahmedabad Rs. 50,000 were given by him for their maintenance ; large sums were paid from the Royal Treasury, and wealthy Nobles contributed for the support of the sufferers. Taxes of every description were abolished for two years. Next year was blessed with rain. After the famine was over no male buffaloes were to be found at Ahmedabad, and one was brought from Champaneer for Rs. 85..</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">" ' II. Hijree 1093, Samvut 1739-40, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1682-83.</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">" ' The people complained of the dearness of grain to the Sooba of Ahmedabad, the rate being one Rupee for 3 or 4 seers. The Soobedar, Mahomed Ameen Khan, on his return with a procession at the ' Eed' holiday found himself surrounded by men, women and children, who complained very bitterly of their condition, and who were instigated by one Shaik Aboo Bukkur to abuse the Soobedar and to throw stones at him. The Soobedar's followers came forward to fight with the people, but he, apprehending a riot, managed to pacify the mob, and to reach home safely. This occurrence was reported to the Emperor, who ordered that those who were engaged in the riot should be killed ; but the Soobedar was an old man, and did not lend himself to such severity. He, however, invited several persons to a feast, and quietly managed to poison Aboo Bukkur, the leader of the mob."</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">" ' III. Hijree 1130, Samvut 1774-75, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1717-18.</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">" ' This famine is called Punchotra. Mut and Bajree were sold 3 or 4 seers for a Rupee. Owing, however, to the Soobedar's good <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-76" data-page="76" data-folio="64" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n75_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 64">[p. 64]</button> management, the people did not suffer very much. Grain was sold to the poor and starving people at the door of Rughoonath Dass Dewan, who contributed much in charity. A child was procurable for a Rupee or two. Rain fell this year, but not seasonably. Epidemic broke out, and destroyed many.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“ ‘ IV.   Hijree 1161, Sumvut 1803-4, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1746-47.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“ ‘ This famine is called ‘ Talotra,’ or ‘ Tankia Kal.’ In this year grass did not grow at all for want of rain. The Soobedar Jawan Murda Khan went into the jungle with his followers and offered prayers, but without success. Grain was sold 3 or 4 seers for a Rupee. People supported themselves with the roots of trees and plants. Dead cattle, birds and men, were used for food. Wells, tanks and rivers, were without water. Many persons went to Malwa and returned when every thing was right; others lost their lives. Several villages were deserted; next year was blessed with rain.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“ ‘ V.   Hijree 1174, Sumvut 1816-17, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1759-60.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“ ‘ This famine was caused by scarcity and war; grain was sold one seer for a Rupee in the Emperor’s army, and one and a half seer in that of the Mahrattas. Other people could get two or three seers for a Rupee. 60,000 men died in the Emperor’s army for want of food.’</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ 3. In Hijree 1205, Sumvut 1847, A. D. 1790-91, there was famine at this place, regarding which the following account is given at page 54 in the History of Surat in Goozeratee, composed by Kavi Nurbuddashunker Lallshunker.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ In Sumvut 1847, A. D. 1791, there was a great famine in Surat, known as the ‘ Soodtallo Kal.’ This famine was very great, owing to the total failure of rain and crops. Prices rose very high. A Rupee would bring only 8 Surat seers of grain, and that even was not procurable in case of purchases to the amount of 50 or 100 Rs. Those who possessed a stock of grain in their shops could not even sell it, because the poor starving people used to plunder it. A supply of grain enough to last about four days could scarcely be had. Bags of Bengal rice were not then imported in sufficient quantity to meet the demand, as ships from Bengal could not make more than one or two trips in a year. Wealthy men of Surat charitably distributed dates, parched jowaree and grain, amongst the poor people, with which and with water the latter supported themselves. Some poor people lived on bran usually eaten by cattle, while others wandered about here and there through the streets, and endeavoured to support themselves by begging waste water prepared for boiling rice food. Some of the respectable people committed suicide by falling into wells and tanks; others threw themselves into the Taptee. Some, indeed, killed their children and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-77" data-page="77" data-folio="65" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n76_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 65">[p. 65]</button> lived on their flesh; gold and silver ornaments were offered at nominal prices, but purchasers could not be found. The heat of the sun was excessive, and the plague broke out. Mortality prevailed to such an extent that corpses were thrown into two large pits, which the Nawab had caused to be dug outside the Surrah and Nowsaree gates. Corpses of poor people were seen lying on the roads. This famine lasted for about a year and a half.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“Syud Hoosein wullud Syud Shariff, Shaik Edroos Saheb, has kindly furnished me with the following information about the above famine:—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“ ‘ Rughoba, a member of the family of Bajee Rao Peishwa, came to Surat from Poona with many persons who suffered from the famine. The grain which was concealed by the merchants was plundered, and this famine is commonly called ‘Ragho Gurdee’s Kal.’ Nawab Nizamoodeen was then Ruler of Surat. When no grain or grass could be procured the cattle were slaughtered, and their flesh was sold very cheap in the streets. The poor people assembled where the cattle were slaughtered and drank the blood, which caused death. Nobody could then think of his religion. Those who were wealthly could get ‘Bundee’ and ‘Kodra’ (inferior kinds of grain), but only with great trouble. This caused heat in the stomach and produced death. Thousands of people thus died, and nobody went to burn or bury them. The poor people eagerly sold their children for 8 annas each. Little infants were given away for nothing to be supported. Millions of souls thus perished. At this time there was some religious ceremony performed in Syud Edroo’s family, but nothing could be procured, except 4 or 5 dry dates, which were rotten. The Rulers of Broach, Baroda, Bombay and Surat, were different individuals, who prevented the export of grain from one city to another. These measures caused great distress.’</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“4. In Sumvut 1860, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1803-4, there was another famine at this place called ‘Sathokal,’ not as severe as the preceding one, and which appears to have taken place from excessive rain. ‘Ghee,’ or clarified butter, was sold very cheap, and the poor people supported themselves with cakes prepared of cow-dung and ‘Ghee.’ This famine took place soon after the British Government obtained possession of Surat, and I found some correspondence about it on our records, copies of which I annex to this. I tried to find out more particulars than what can be gathered from the correspondence in question on the subject, but without success.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Sumvut 1888, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1831-32. Sumvut 1895. <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1838-39.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">“5. The records of our Department shew that want of sufficient and timely rain occasioned some scarcity in this Zillah during the years quoted in the margin; consequently, remissions of assessment to the extent of Rs: 3,70,397 were granted in the former year, and of Rs. 4,96,121-12-8 <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-78" data-page="78" data-folio="66" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n77_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 66">[p. 66]</button> were granted in the latter year. The Statement of the current prices of certain articles in this Collectorate in the years from <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1824 to <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1863 inclusive (forwarded with the late Collector Mr. Rogers' letter No. 1486, of the 5th December 1863, to Mr. Showell, Secretary to the Commission to collect information on the subject of prices), however, clearly shews that before the year <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1860 grain and other articles of food were during the above period of nearly 36 years, including the aforesaid two years of scarcity, never so dear as from <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1860 up to the present time."</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">40. The correspondence referred to at the conclusion of paragraph 4 as being on the Surat records is thus given :—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"<span class="sc">To Edward Galley</span>, Esq., Collector at Surat. <span class="sc">Sir</span>, I am directed to acquaint you that, in consideration of the circumstances of the present times, the Honorable the Governor in Council has been pleased to grant a remission of Customs for the ensuing three months on all importations of grain into Surat by sea, to encourage the merchants to venture their vessels across the Gulf to that Port. I have, &amp;c., (Signed) J. A. GRANT, <em>Secretary to Government. Bombay Castle, 14th July</em> 1803.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p><span class="sc">To James Augustus Grant</span>, Esq., Secretary to Government, <span class="sc">Bombay</span>. <span class="sc">Sir</span>, I have had the honor to receive your letter of the 14th instant, advising that the Honorable the Governor in Council has been pleased to grant a remission of Customs, for the ensuing three months, on all importations of grain into this place by sea, and to which injunction all attention shall be paid. I have, &amp;c., (Signed) E. GALLEY, <em>Collector</em>. <em>Surat Collector's Office, 25th July</em> 1803.</p></blockquote></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-79"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="79" data-folio="67" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n78_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 67">[p. 67]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2">To KESHOWRAM SOBHARAM, ` Amuldar of the Hansote Pergunnah.</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am informed by Mr. Crow that some of the merchants of this place have been to Hansote to purchase and bring here a quantity of grain, and that you had prevented them from exporting the same. I therefore write this to you, and direct that in future you do not stop any grain actually purchased for this market; but you must not permit the exportation of this article to any other place, Broach excepted.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Surat, the 18th September</em> 1803.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">To EDWARD GALLEY, Esq., Collector of Surat.</h3><h4 class="sec-h3">SIR,</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In advising you of the great distress which this Settlement is now labouring under from unexpected scarcity of rice, the Governor in Council desires me to intimate that it has become extremely desirable that as much of the Government share of that growing in the Pergunnah of Chicklee and the other Pergunnahs lately ceded by the Peishwa, or otherwise throughout the Attaveesee, as you and the Magistrate at Surat may think can be spared from the local consumption of that city, should be consigned as early as possible to the use of Government at the Presidency, being at the same time instructed to suggest that in such case the export need not be immediately made from Surat, but rather from the port of Bulsar, or any other convenient Bunder on the coast to which the rice might, with all possible expedition, be transported by land, and thence embarked without occasioning any unnecessary alarm to the inhabitants of Surat, or giving occasion to interested dealers thence to raise the price in that market.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">2. If there be any other grains applicable to the sustenance of the natives of this part of the coast which can, in your own and Mr. Crow’s opinions, more safely admit of export than rice, this will prove a very acceptable aid to Government; and, besides what may be thus available from the Attaveesee, you are authorized and directed to endeavour to procure, by contract or other mode of purchase, as much wheat, rice, or bajree, as can be found for export in any of the ports on the opposite side of the Gulf, or from any part within the peninsula of Goozerat, taking care that the purchases be not made in the name of Government, as that might tend unnecessarily to raise the price, and discourage other merchants, which is by no means the intention of Government, whose sole object is to procure <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-80" data-page="80" data-folio="68" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n79_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 68">[p. 68]</button> the greatest practicable supply in the shortest possible period, with a view to answer the immediate exigencies of this Settlement, and the eventual, but highly probable, ones of the army above the Ghauts.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have, &amp;c.,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(Signed) J. A. GRANT, Secretary to Government.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Bombay Castle, 14th October</em> 1803.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">To J. A. Grant</span>, Esq., Secretary to Government.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">BOMBAY.</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have the honor to acknowledge the receipt on the 19th instant of your letter, in duplicate, of the 14th, in this Department, and request you will assure the Honorable the Governor in Council of my speedy and prompt attention to the orders therein conveyed.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have, &amp;c.,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(Signed) E. GALLEY, Collector.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">To Edward Galley</span>, Esq., Agent of Government,</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">SURAT.</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">After the fullest enquiry into the subject of our late conference, namely, the degree of supply to be spared from the grain the produce of these Purgunnahs, I am apprehensive that the necessities of the country cannot be furnished till the next harvest, and that with the best economy and mangement much severe distress must ensue to the city of Surat and its dependencies, unless some foreign resources can be obtained. I beg leave, therefore, to offer it as my clear opinion that no exportation whatever of grain should be permitted from Panura to the Nurbudda, and I request that you will be pleased to issue orders to the different Kumavisdars to assist my arrangements for preventing the same.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have, &amp;c.,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(Signed) N. CROW, J. and M.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Surat, 2nd November</em> 1803.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-81"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="81" data-folio="69" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n80_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 69">[p. 69]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">To J. A. Grant</span>, <span class="sc">Esq.</span>, Secretary to Government, BOMBAY.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir,</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">For the purpose of more speedily forwarding the instructions of Government conveyed through you under date the 14th ultimo, and enabling them more expeditiously to meet the exigency likely to be imposed on them from the expected deficiency and scarcity of grain at the Presidency, I availed myself of the assistance of Nagurdass Kursondass, and employed him in aiding the execution of the commission with which I was entrusted.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">By advices recently received from his Agents at Bhownagur, it appears that he had succeeded in already purchasing 623 Culseys at from 18¼ to 18½ the Surat maund of bajree, and 85 Culseys of wheat, as per two lists enclosed, and that he was procuring a further supply, from which he was obliged to desist owing to some difficulty arising on the part of the Raja of that place, who objected to the exportation of what he had in the first instance procured; in consequence of this notification I addressed a letter to the Raja in Nagur's favor, which will, I trust, be productive of the desired effect, and in the meanwhile the Agent has been instructed to refrain from further purchases until the sanction of the Raja has been obtained to admit of its exportation.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have the pleasure to advise that 8 boats, with their contents as per list herewith transmitted, <em>viz.</em> 5 at Billimora and 3 at Bulsar, are now waiting at those ports to take advantage of the first cruiser that can be procured to convey them to Bombay. It was my intention that a part of these boats should have sailed last Thursday under the protection of the ‘Princess Royal,’ which was liberated merely by the extreme tempestuousness of the weather, and which obliged the boats that were hardly enough to attempt joining the convoy on that day in spite of the fury of the gale to return to the bar.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In consequence of the enclosed letter from the Judge and Magistrate at this place, I have resolved to put a stop to the purchase in the Attaveesee and exportation of more grain than what is already procured.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have, &amp;c.,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(Signed) E. GALLEY, <em>Collector.</em> .</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Surat Collector's Office, 7th November</em> 1803.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The detail of the contents of the boats, &amp;c., are here given.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-82"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="82" data-folio="70" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n81_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 70">[p. 70]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">To</span> J. A. <span class="sc">Grant, Esq.</span>, Secretary to Government, BOMBAY.</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>, I have the honor to advise that the eight boats containing grain from the Chicklee and Bulsar Pergunnahs, noticed in my letter of the 7th instant, now proceed under convoy of the Honorable Cruiser 'Princess Augusta,' and I shall do myself the honor to transmit by to-morrow's Dawk, if possible, the invoice of the above mentioned grain. I have, &amp;c.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(Signed) E. GALLEY, <em>Collector.</em></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Surat Collector's Office, 9th November</em> 1803.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">To</span> J. A. <span class="sc">Grant, Esq.</span>, Secretary to Government, BOMBAY.</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>, Herewith I have the honor to enclose a list of boats loaded with grain, on the Honorable Company's account, imported from Bhownagur, which have been put under convoy of the 'Princess Augusta,' and will sail from hence for the Presidency to-morrow. The Commander of the Cruiser above named has also been instructed to take under his protection two Bottilas here undermentioned in the event of their arriving at the bar previous to his getting under weigh, which there is some reason to expect may be the case. These Bottilas are from Dholera, and likewise contain a cargo of grain on account of the Honorable Company. I have, &amp;c.,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(Signed) E. GALLEY, <em>Collector.</em></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Surat Collector's Office, 30th November</em> 1803.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">To</span> J. A. <span class="sc">Grant, Esq.</span>, Secretary to Government, BOMBAY.</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>, I have the honor to advise that four boats loaded with grain on the Honorable Company's account have sailed for the Presidency since my <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-83" data-page="83" data-folio="71" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n82_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 71">[p. 71]</button> communication of the 30th ultimo, as per list herewith enclosed. Two of these boats are those which I mentioned in my letter above alluded to as likely to have sailed under convoy of the ‘Princess Augusta,’ but it subsequently appeared that they did not arrive at the bar in time to take advantage of that opportunity to proceed to their destination.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have, &amp;c.,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(Signed)  E. GALLEY, Collector.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Surat Collector's Office, 19th December</em> 1803.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">To Edward Galley</span>, Esq., Collector of Surat.</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir,</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The city being greatly distressed for want of the accession of the supplies of the new harvest, and the Banjarras collected in great number on the skirts of the Attaveesee to carry off the produce to foreign parts, I have urgently to request that, as soon as the Jummabundy may be settled and permission given to sell the crops, the Kumavisdars, Dessaces and Patels, may be instructed to encourage, as much as possible, the importation of the grain here, and, with the Chowkeedars and Nakadars, receive a general injunction not to permit the exportation of any quantity out of these Pergunnahs, either by land or by sea.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have, &amp;c.,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(Signed)  NATHAN CROW, Judge and Magistrate.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Surat, the 8th January</em> 1804.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">To Nathan Crow</span>, Esq., Judge and Magistrate,</h4><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Surat</span>.</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir,</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have the honor to acknowledge the receipt of your letter of yesterday’s date, in reply to which I have to observe that no time will be lost in completing the settlement of the Jummabundy. The Kumavisdars have <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-84" data-page="84" data-folio="72" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n83_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 72">[p. 72]</button> already been instructed to prevent the exportation of grain from the Attaveesee to foreign parts.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have, &amp;c.,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(Signed) <span class="sc">E. GALLEY,</span> <em>Collector.</em></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Surat Collector's Office, the 9th January</em> 1804.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">To J. A. <span class="sc">Grant</span>, Esq., Secretary to Government, BOMBAY.</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir,</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have the honor to transmit you herewith an invoice of the grain consigned by me to the Presidency since my letter of the 17th November last.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have, &amp;c.,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(Signed) <span class="sc">E. GALLEY,</span> <em>Collector.</em></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Surat Collector's Office, the 6th February</em> 1868.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">To <span class="sc">Edwaed Galley</span>, Esq., Collector of Surat.</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir,</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Since my last addresses to you respecting grain, the difficulties on that score have, in spite of all my endeavours, so much increased that I am under the necessity of again troubling you on the subject, and of urgently soliciting the favor of your injunctions on your officers and people in the Purgunnahs to co-operate with mine in preventing exportation, and also in drawing the supplies of the country towards the city.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The allurement of advantage which the indigent state of the country to the southward and eastward presents to exportation from these Purgunnahs has invited vast numbers of Bunjarras to visit them in order to purchase grain, and the same temptation of profit has, I am sorry to say, corrupted those from whose faithful vigilance alone an effective check was to be relied on for preserving to the land the blessings of its own productions, namely, the Amils.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-85"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="85" data-folio="73" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n84_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 73">[p. 73]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Finding that the grain was fast ebbing out of the country, I endeavoured by stationing people of my own near the Southern Bunders, and in the neighbourhood of the different passes up the Ghauts, to stop the exhaustion, and I wrote to the Rajas of Bansda, of Dhurumpoor, of Mandvee, and also to the Guicowar's Sirsoobedar at Nowsarce, to excite their co-operation in my object. I exerted all the influence in my power likewise over the farmer of the tolls between the Nurbudda and Panura to engage his good offices, but his interest was too incompatible with such views as mine to make more zeal and sincerity probable than what I have experienced. The only aid that has availed me was that of Colonel Anderson while he was in the field. Notwithstanding, however, the inadequacy of means, and the great difficulty of checking such an extensive range of country, the exportation has been much discouraged, but the cupidity lighted up is not subdued, for, disappointed at present of carrying the grain without the Pergunnahs, the owners are hoarding it in the Peishwa's and Guicowar's villages, or secreting it in mines in their own, till more favorable opportunity may offer of carrying it abroad, or similar misery at Home hold out equal glut to their avarice.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The state of the city is, therefore, not relieved by any thing I have yet been able to effect. The check to exportation must be completed, and the hoards opened to the internal consumption, and this I am sanguine in thinking may be accomplished if you will be kind enough to issue general directions to your people, of whatever description and wherever stationed in the Pergunnahs, to render assistance to mine in all measures they may pursue (and for which I am of course alone answerable) towards preventing the exportation of grain, and driving it to the city.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The salt trade is one of the spacious covers under which the trade in grain is masked, and I am aware that many plausible arguments of discouragement to useful merchants, diminution of a branch of revenue, and danger of losing it altogether, will be urged to you to establish the impolicy of subjecting the Bunjarras to check. I have not overlooked a consideration of such importance as the Company's revenue, and I am satisfied that none of the measures I have in view for the subsistence of the people are at all injurious to the Nimuksaree Mahal, but if the interests of the one and the other were at variance, I should not hesitate to give it as my humble opinion that the Company had better lose double its value than the country be exposed to the horrors of famine and the city to insurrection, which are the dangers that threaten now.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have, &amp;c.,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(Signed) NATHAN CROW,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Judge and Magistrate.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Surat, 15th February</em> 1804.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-86"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="86" data-folio="74" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n85_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 74">[p. 74]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">To Nathan Crow</span>, Esq., Judge and Magistrate, SURAT.</h3><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have the honor to acknowledge the receipt of your letter of yesterday's date, and, in reply, to assure you that I shall cordially contribute every assistance in my power to alleviate, in conjunction with yourself, the calamities complained of, in view to which the injunctions you have suggested shall be promptly communicated to the Amuldars and other Revenue Officers in the different Pergunnahs, and their obedience thereto strictly enacted.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have, &amp;c.,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(Signed) E. GALLEY, Collector.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Surat Collector's Office, the 16th February 1804.</em></p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">To Edward Galley</span>, Esq., Collector at Surat.</h3><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am directed by the Honorable the Governor in Council to desire that you afford the fullest aid and support that your Department is susceptible of to the measures recommended in the Judge and Magistrate's letter to you of the 8th of January and 15th of February last.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">All duties on the import of grain into Surat are to be taken off till further orders.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have, &amp;c.,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(Signed) J. A. GRANT, Secretary to Government.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Bombay Castle, 21st February 1804.</em></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">This day an advertisement was published for the remission of the duties on grain until further orders.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">This is to give notice that, in consequence of the directions of Government, all duties on the importation of grain will cease from this date.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have, &amp;c.,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(Signed) E.
