GALLEY, Collector."</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Surat Collector's Office, the 26th February 1804.</em></p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-87"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="87" data-folio="75" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n86_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 75">[p. 75]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>From the District Deputy Collector, dated 11th October.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">"<span class="sc">Famine Report</span> of the Chowrasee Purgunnah, submitted in accordance with the instructions contained in the Surat Collector's endorsement No. 1673, of the 7th October 1867.</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-020"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-020 · FOLIO 87</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/report-past-famines-bombay-etheridge-1868/datasets/tables/tbl-020_p0087.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/report-past-famines-bombay-etheridge-1868/datasets/tidy/tbl-020_p0087.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><thead><tr><th scope="col">Sumvut Year.</th><th scope="col">Corresponding Christian Year.</th><th scope="col">Particulars.</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">1847</td><td class="num">1791</td><td>This famine is generally known by the name of "Soodtala" (<em>i. e.</em> appertaining to 1847), derived from the Sumvut year in which it took place. Grain was sold at the rate of 15 seers (Suratee) per Rupee. The people were in great distress owing to the scarcity of grain. Their sufferings, however, were alleviated by the large supply of cocoanuts and dates shipped from other ports, and sold at a cheap rate. This information is traditional, and is obtained from Dajee Rughoonath Dasaee.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">1860</td><td class="num">1804</td><td>In this year the harvest was good, but the dearness of grain was caused by the Duknees, who flocked to this part of the country in consequence of the famine which ravaged the Deccan in that year. The consequence was that grain rose to from 16 to 13 seers (Suratee) per Rupee. This year of scarcity is generally known by the name of "Satho."</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">1869</td><td class="num">1813</td><td>Scarcity caused by the invasion of the Pindarees. The price of grain was from 25 to 22 seers (Suratee) per Rupee.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">1880</td><td class="num">1824</td><td>Scarcity caused by the western part (of India) having been desolated. The price of corn ranged from 30 to 28 seers (Suratee) per Rupee.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">1895</td><td class="num">1839</td><td>Scarcity of grain caused by insufficient fall of rain. Grain was sold at from 24 to 22 seers (Suratee) per Rupee.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">1920</td><td class="num">1864</td><td>This year of dearness may be compared with the "Soodtala" (famine of Sumvut 1847), grain having risen from 15 to 13 seers (Suratee) per Rupee.</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>General Remarks.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The means adopted for the relief of the people suffering from the old famines cannot be ascertained from the persons now living. As none of the persons who have out-lived the famines are forthcoming, the information was gathered from Shiva Bhonla of Kuttargaum, Dajee Rughoonathjee of Delsoda, and Ravil Nundsal Dullal through the medium of Rajaram Rughoonath."</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">41. From Candeish the Acting Collector, Mr. Sheppard, who had only recently taken charge on the departure of the Collector, Mr. Ashburner, for England, reported, on the 2nd April last, as follows:—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">"2. I regret to report that the information forthcoming on the subject of past famines in this province is somewhat meagre, and reaches only as far back as the commencement of the present century. The famine which then occurred was an important era in Candeish history.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-88"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="88" data-folio="76" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n87_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 76">[p. 76]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“3. The province at that time belonged to the Peishwa, the East India Company having a small share in the revenue. Both Scindia and Holkur owned considerable estates in the province. In the troubles which ensued on Bajeerow’s elevation to the Peishwa’s ‘Gaddee,’ and the consequent hostility of the above princes, the then fertile and well-populated province of Candeish suffered, perhaps, even more than any part of the Deccan.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“4. There is no reason to suppose that the season of 1802-3 was an unfavorable one, or that any scarcity in neighbouring provinces had caused emigration from them. The country was probably at the height of its prosperity. Water was abundant. Population, even in the now almost deserted tracts beneath the Sattpooras, was considerable;<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-03-fn-002" href="#sec-03-fn-002" data-fn-target="sec-03-fn-002" role="doc-noteref">*</a></sup> at this time it was that two seasons of anarchy and lawlessness spread desolation and famine through these districts.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“5. It is not necessary for me to describe how the followers of the two great Chiefs wasted, destroyed, and looted Candeish during this period; how the Pindaree bands sacked and burnt villages in every direction, even destroying the grain standing in the fields; and how the Bheels had their turn in the general plunder. There are still some living in Candeish who remember these times, and the almost equally distressing period which immediately succeeded them.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“6. The famine which was the consequence probably decimated Candeish. Grain sold at less than 1 seer per Rupee. Such was the disturbed state of the Deccan, and indeed of the whole of this part of India, that none could be imported from without. Vast numbers died from famine, or consequent disease, and many others left their ruined homes never to return to them, and either deserted the province altogether or settled down in the more populous parts. Terrible accounts of the severity of the famine are given by some of the Mamlutdars.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“7. The Government of the Peishwa appears to have done something to meet the emergency of the case. The Vinchoorker was appointed as a special officer to carry out remedial measures. Duties on imports were abolished, revenue and cesses were remitted. Export of grain was prohibited, and the prices of the necessaries of life were regulated. Measures were likewise adopted to put a stop to the freebooting of the Arabs, Bheels, &amp;c.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-89"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="89" data-folio="77" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n88_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 77">[p. 77]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“8. By the end of the year 1804 the country appears to have again become quiet, but the effects of the previous fifteen months are still visible in many parts of the province.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Amount of remission of revenue Rs. 7,33,913, being Rs. 49-12-9 per cent. of the revenue for collection.</aside></div><blockquote><p>“9. The year 1824-25 was one of great scarcity. It is reported that no rain whatever, except a few slight showers, fell that season in Candeish. The seasons immediately preceding and succeeding it appear to have been favourable ones, so that, although considerable distress was experienced by the poorer classes, Government do not appear to have been called upon for any special assistance beyond remission of the yearly revenue.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Amount of remission of revenue Rs. 3,61,677 and Rs. 6,47,845 in those years respectively, being Rs. 24-1-3 and Rs 37-12-2 per cent. of the revenue for collection.</aside></div><blockquote><p>“10. The years 1832 and 1845 are also reported to have been seasons of great scarcity and distress, though hardly to be called famine years. Insufficient or unseasonable falls of rain are said to have been the causes of the bad harvests to which the said distress was attributable. Considerable remissions were granted by Government.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“11. There is no very reliable record of the prices of grain during the years last mentioned. It is, however, tolerably certain that these, though high enough to cause considerable suffering, were not as high as those obtained in the years from 1862—66. During this period not only were the monsoons in this province unfavourable, but the area sown with cereals was very much reduced, owing to the extravagant prices obtained for cotton owing to the American war. The temptation to sow cotton was too strong to be resisted, and but for the Railway and other public works, which were the means of providing abundance of work at liberal rates of wages, the lower classes would have suffered considerably.”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">42. From Ahmednuggur, the Acting Collector, Mr. Erskine, wrote as follows on the 30th August:—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“1. I have made the enquiries required by your Circular on the subject of famines in these districts, and have the honor to send you the following details, which, meagre as they are, are all I have been able to obtain.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“2. One reason it is impossible to give exact details is the satisfactory one that there has been no general famine, indeed nothing deserving the name of a famine, since the country has passed under British rule.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“3. In former times unfortunately there were from time to time famines. I do not allude to traditions, such, for instance, as the tradition of a terrible famine nearly 400 years ago, for, of course, nothing is known of this, the tradition merely being that for twelve years there was drought, and the result was that famine was sore in the land. There are, indeed, no <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-90" data-page="90" data-folio="78" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n89_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 78">[p. 78]</button> records which give any details of any famines previous to the British occupation, and I am unable to give you any information concerning them.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote is-locus">1792.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">There is, however, no doubt that in the year <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1792 there was in the more southern and western parts of this Collectorate—in the Jamkhier, Parnair, Akola, Sungumnair, &amp;c., districts—great misery and want owing to the price of grain, which is said at that time to have been selling at six seers the Rupee, a rate which, considering the value of money at that period and the usual price at which the cheaper sorts of grain sold then, would be equivalent, probably, to grain selling now at three or four seers a Rupee. I have been unable to ascertain particulars as to what loss of life resulted from this scarcity, if there was any, and have thought it unnecessary to delay while endeavouring to procure details, as there will probably be no difficulty in ascertaining all particulars from the Poona Duftur.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“4. I may, indeed, here mention that all enquiries have been met by the same reply from Chiefs and Hereditary District Officers that their papers, public as well as private, were taken away by the Inam Commission, and have never been returned. Even the Chief of Vinchoor, to whom, among others, I applied, stated he had no papers of older date than 40 years back, and that he could not obtain detailed accounts from any of the hereditary Officers in the villages forming his estate. Probably, therefore, all the statistical information that is procurable will be found in the records now in the possession of the Alienation Department, and I shall therefore, without attempting to provide statistics, proceed to record the general information I have succeeded in collecting.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote is-locus">1803-4.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">“5. The only general famine in these districts of which I have been able to hear any details was the one of <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1803-4, and from hearsay evidence there can be no doubt that there was great loss of life. Old men of good standing, with whom I have conversed on the subject, estimate the loss of life in the town of Ahmednuggur alone at ‘about 5,000 or 6,000 souls,’ but whether this estimate is well founded I have no means of ascertaining, as I have not been able to procure any documentary evidence on the subject.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“6. This famine was in a few districts partly caused by a bad season, but even in these the season was not so bad as to have led to a famine had it not been for the excesses of the soldiery who accompanied Holkur, and of the Pindarees who accompanied his army when they invaded the country in that year. The crops of that year were never allowed to come to maturity; they were carried off green to feed the elephants, horses, &amp;c., that accompanied the different armies. Much too was uselessly destroyed. All the grain stores too were plundered, and thus not only were the people deprived of what was intended as a reserve for a bad season, but they were <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-91" data-page="91" data-folio="79" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n90_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 79">[p. 79]</button> left without sufficient grain to sow their fields when the sowing season came round, and thus the misery and want was prolonged.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“7. The following account of this famine is given by Mr. Krishnarao Vittul Vinchoorkur, the details having, he states, been gathered from men ‘of strict veracity, some of whom were grown up men at the time of the famine, and quite competent to form intelligent opinions on the things passing around them.’ He writes as follows (and I give his account in extenso):—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“ ‘Jeshwuntrow Holkur, burning for revenge for the cruel and treacherous murder of his brother Vittojee Holkur by the orders of the Peishwa Bajeerow II, resolved in that year to proceed to wreak a terrible vengeance on the perpetrators of so dreadful a crime. He was followed by a host of armed men, irregular horse, foot soldiers, Pindarees, &amp;c., nearly all allured to his standard by the desire of plunder. Jeshwuntrow commenced by plundering and devastating the Peishwa’s territories on this side of the river Nurbudda, and extended his depredations through the intermediate country as far as the river Krishna. Two of Jeshwuntrow’s officers, namely, Futtehsing Manay and Shah Ahmed Khan Pathan, swept, like wild-fire, all the villages lying on both sides of the river Godavery. Clouds of horsemen and Pindarees were to be observed in all directions under different leaders, committing with perfect impunity the work of destruction, pillaging and burning villages, extorting from people the heaviest contributions, either in money or corn, while their horses and bullocks devoured extensive standing crops of unripe grain, destroying at the same time what they could not devour. Large stores of corn were forced open, and their contents consumed or thrown away, just as the grain happened to be good or bad. These and similar atrocities naturally brought on a famine of the worst type. The fields had nothing to yield; the stores of corn intended to serve as safe-guards against hard times were empty. The cry for food was loud and appalling, but no response could be given to it. Corn was not to be had but at an enormously high price. Wheat could be got at half a seer per Rupee. Even at this price it was not obtainable at all places, but when it was obtained by some means or other it was difficult to carry it home, for people concealed themselves by way-side and pounced upon any one who happened to pass that way with a small quantity of corn, snatched it out of his grasp, and sometimes murdered him if he resisted. The calamity grew on till it could grow no longer, and assumed an appearance that was awful to contemplate. People had recourse to the vegetables which the earth spontaneously produces. My informants narrate the most horrifying details of the way in which people lived at this stage of the famine. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-92" data-page="92" data-folio="80" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n91_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 80">[p. 80]</button> Those who could afford purchased a small quantity of rotten wheat, ground them, and mixing the flour with a very large proportion of vegetables, to mitigate in some degree its noisome stench, made a bread out of the compound; others less fortunate collected a quantity of the young and tender leaves of the Tamarind tree, and mixing them with a kind of white earth the mass was pounded to the consistency of paste, and made into a sort of jelly by being boiled with water. Here and there instances occurred which showed to what a miserably low level human nature may descend at times of such extreme helplessness. Men destroyed and devoured their infants, little innocent girls were sold for a few Rupees to professional prostitutes, high caste Hindoos ate of the flesh of the sacred cow, and Moosulmans devoured the detestable pigs. Thousands died of unwholesome food, and tens of thousands of no food. In large towns streets were strewn with the dead and dying, and the survivors were too weak, or too exhausted, to carry the dead to be burnt on the funeral pile, or to be buried in the graves. Smaller villages fared the worst of all. Many of them were entirely depopulated, not one of their inhabitants having survived to tell the mournful tale.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">" 'Such is a faint outline of the distress which prevailed during the great famine, which continued with unabated fury for upwards of three months, and produced the most frightful results.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">" 'The country afflicted by this dire calamity lay between the river Nurbudda on the North, the river Krishna on the South, the Syanhadri range on the West, and the Nizam's dominions on the East.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">" 'The afflicted district was at the time under no settled Government. It had one ruler to-day and another to-morrow. Anarchy in its worst form stalked over the length and breadth of that unfortunate region. Relief from rulers was, therefore, out of the question. Here and there a wealthy Sowkar, or a pious Brahman, doled out in miserable pittance a quantity of grain to relatives, friends, or sometimes to a few passers-by, but this relief could not reach the starving masses. Those who could bear the fatigues of long journey emigrated therefore into Goozerat, where corn was plentiful and cheap, and thus saved their lives.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">" 'In the midst of all this wide-spread desolation there was one consolation. It was not the failure of water that had caused the famine; the rains were copious, the rivers, tanks, wells, all were full. There was plenty of grass for cattle, and their milk afforded some relief to the people. Gradually corn found its way into the forlorn district. Good roads there were none, and carts could not be used, but the 'Wunjarees,' —a wandering, but in those days a useful, tribe—imported vast quantities <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-93" data-page="93" data-folio="81" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n92_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 81">[p. 81]</button> of corn from Goozerat by means of their bullocks. Distress diminished proportionately. Village people who had fled to distances returned to their houses. Fields were resown, and kind nature rushed to the rescue of her unfortunate children. Land yielded an uncommonly large return, corn became plentiful and within the reach of the poorest, and the face of the country once more smiled with joy.’</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“8. The above account, even if there are a few touches in it for effect, shows accurately the popular tradition concerning the famine.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“9. It is hardly necessary for me to remind you that it was in August of the year 1803 that Ahmednuggur was taken by the troops commanded by Major General Wellesley. The 9th of August was the date of the capture of the fort, and on the 13th he complained to the Government of the want of forage. ‘The service cannot be carried on in this manner; the troops must have regular supplies of provisions at command, or misfortune and disgrace will be the result.’</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“10. I may here mention that when the fort was taken a large quantity of grain was found stored inside, and this was used to pay large numbers of half-starving people, who were employed in throwing up the glacis round the fort ditch, and is still mentioned as having been the means of helping, and probably saving, large numbers of the poorer classes.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“11. Major General Wellesley again on the 23rd April 1804, when alluding to the scarcity in these districts, writes to the Government of Bombay—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“ ‘Your Excellency is, I imagine, aware of the scarcity which prevails throughout the Deccan, and which has produced a famine. By a report which I have received from the Commander of the Mysore Horse, whom I had sent with his corps to forage in districts on the Nizam’s frontier, which were supposed not to have suffered equally with those farther to the westward, I learn that the distress is extreme; that he can scarcely procure forage or grain for his horses; that that which is procured is at an enormous expense; that he had been some days without food, and that he had lost 100 horses in one day. The horses of the British Cavalry have been for some time eating rice at Poona, a food which, it is well known, is unwholesome for them; but as that is the only grain which Bombay could supply, and as the grain for horses and cattle could always, till now, be procured in the country, it is the only grain in the magazines which I have collected at Poona and Ahmednuggur for the troops.’</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“12. No part of the country seems to have suffered more during this famine than the Peint State. Mr. J. MacFarlan, the Assistant Collector in charge of the State, writes—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“ ‘In the Peint State this famine was very severe, and lasted for two years. No measures of relief appear to have been adopted as a <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-94" data-page="94" data-folio="82" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n93_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 82">[p. 82]</button> general rule. In Peint attempts to import grain were made, but the Bheels always carried it off.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“ ‘ Since the English took the country there has been no serious famine. In 1824 there was considerable distress for about four months, probably May, June, July and August, caused by failure of the monsoon, grain rising in price to 8 seers for the Rupee. There is no record as to deaths, but it is not supposed that there were many anywhere but in Peint, where the deaths are estimated at 1,200. Measures of relief do not appear to have been adopted.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“ ‘ Again in 1845-46 the fall of rain was short ; grain fell to 4 and even 3 pailees (12 seers) per Rupee, but the distress did not last longer than six months, and it is not known that deaths occurred except at Peint, where about 1000 persons died in spite of an importation of grain.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“ ‘ There was scarcity again in Peint in 1854, when grain fell to 3 pailees per Rupee, and 500 persons died.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“ ‘ I have looked up the English records, but find nothing of importance regarding famines in them.’</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“ 13. There is nothing on record, so far as I have been able to discover in this Office, concerning these famines and the loss of life in Peint, but as at that time Nassick was a Sub-Collectorate the want may be accounted for.”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">43. Mr. Erskine also forwarded the following Statement and extra reports on 9th January last, at the same time regretting that, though he and the Assistant Collectors had during their tours made every enquiry, no further information was forthcoming.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-95"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="95" data-folio="83" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n94_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 83">[p. 83]</button></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-021"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-021 · FOLIO 95</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/report-past-famines-bombay-etheridge-1868/datasets/tables/tbl-021_p0095.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/report-past-famines-bombay-etheridge-1868/datasets/tidy/tbl-021_p0095.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><caption><em>Statement showing the rates at which grain was sold at Khurda, a well-known trading town in the Jamkheir Talooka of the Ahmednuggur Collectorate, during the famine which is said to have taken place during the years</em> 1803 <em>and</em> 1804, <em>found in the account books of Babajee Siwalal Marwaree and Vittul Bapoojce Takbhatay.</em></caption><thead><tr><th scope="col">Hindoo Dates.</th><th scope="col">Toor Dall. Seers per Rupee.</th><th scope="col">Wheat. Seers per Rupee.</th><th scope="col">Gram. Seers per Rupee.</th><th scope="col">Bajree. Seers per Rupee.</th><th scope="col">Jowaree. Seers per Rupee.</th><th scope="col">Moong. Seers per Rupee.</th><th scope="col">Ooreed. Seers per Rupee.</th><th scope="col">Remarks.</th></tr><tr><th scope="col">1</th><th scope="col">2</th><th scope="col">3</th><th scope="col">4</th><th scope="col">5</th><th scope="col">6</th><th scope="col">7</th><th scope="col">8</th><th scope="col">9</th></tr><tr><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col" colspan="6"><em>Sumvut year</em> 1860, <em>corresponding with A. D.</em> 1803.</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>Vyshak Wudya 8th</td><td>......</td><td class="num">21 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub></td><td class="num">19 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub></td><td>......</td><td>......</td><td>......</td><td>......</td><td></td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Jesht Shood 5th</td><td>......</td><td class="num">17 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub></td><td>......</td><td class="num">23</td><td>......</td><td>......</td><td>......</td><td></td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Ashad Shood 5th</td><td>......</td><td <sup>1</sup>/<sub>4</sub></td><td>......</td><td class="num">1 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>4</sub></td><td class="num">1 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>4</sub></td><td>......</td><td>......</td><td></td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Bhadrapud ” 2nd</td><td>......</td><td>......</td><td class="num">3 <sup>3</sup>/<sub>4</sub></td><td>......</td><td class="num">3 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>4</sub></td><td class="num">7</td><td>......</td><td class="num">4 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>4</sub></td><td></td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Ponsh ” 6th</td><td>......</td><td>......</td><td>......</td><td class="num">6 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>4</sub></td><td>......</td><td>......</td><td class="num">5</td><td></td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Magh ” 4th</td><td>Toor 7 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub></td><td>......</td><td>......</td><td class="num">7 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub></td><td>......</td><td>......</td><td>......</td><td></td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>From Yadow Dhondeo, Deshpanday of Purgunnah Chandore.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ Before the English reign in <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1804, about 63 years ago, Jeshwuntrao Holkur, with a host of an army numbering from one to one and half lacs, crossed this en route to Poona. They looted the whole of the villages on the way. The Pindarees also plundered as they pleased. That year the monsoon was favourable and the crops excellent. But the standing crops were destroyed by Holkur’s men; granaries of corn also were looted. This caused the desertion of villages. Grain was sold at <sup>3</sup>/<sub>4</sub> seer per Rupee, and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-96" data-page="96" data-folio="84" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n95_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 84">[p. 84]</button> great famine was the result. The famine lasted one year. Many people subsisted on vegetables, sweet potatoes, a kind of seed called ‘Bud-bud,’ and on milk and curds.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“The second famine occurred in <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1824. This was the result of the absence of rain, which prevented the growth of crops. Water became scarce, and grain was sold at enhanced rates, which the poor people had not the means of buying.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“From the ‘Kird’ belonging to Bruhmagiree Juggunnath, Deshpanday, it appears the prices ranged as follows:—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-022"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-022 · FOLIO 96</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/report-past-famines-bombay-etheridge-1868/datasets/tables/tbl-022_p0096.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/report-past-famines-bombay-etheridge-1868/datasets/tidy/tbl-022_p0096.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><thead><tr><th scope="col" colspan="3">“In <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1804 —</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>“Bajree</td><td class="num">3½</td><td>seers for the Rupee.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>“Jowaree</td><td class="num">3½</td><td>ditto.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>“Rice</td><td class="num">3</td><td>ditto.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>“Gram</td><td class="num">3</td><td>ditto.</td></tr><tr class="row-subheader"><td colspan="3">“In <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1824—</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>“Bajree</td><td class="num">10</td><td>seers for the Rupee.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>“Wheat</td><td class="num">10</td><td>ditto</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>“Gram</td><td class="num">9½</td><td>ditto</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>“Ooreed</td><td class="num">10</td><td>ditto</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>“Toor</td><td class="num">11</td><td>ditto</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“The people who saw the famine in 1804, and who are now alive, all assert that grain was sold at ¾ seer, but there is no written record about this. Many people died that year for want of food. The number said to have died averaged about two-thirds in each village. The total number of deaths might have been between 7,000 and 9,000. Influential and liberal Sowcars tried to save lives by distributing ¼ seer grain to each man. Others made and gave a loaf to each. The Peishwa’s rule was in force, but the Peishwa did not make any arrangements for affording relief. Some state that the English Government brought jowaree and sold it in the famine-stricken districts.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“In <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1824 people are not said to have died, but great distress prevailed owing to the inability of the poor to purchase grain at the enhanced prices. No relief was afforded. Merchants imported grain from cheap countries, and sold it to the inhabitants. This year remission was allowed by Government.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>From Dajee Narayen, Deshpanday of Talooka Sinnur.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“There were two famines, one in <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1793-94 and another in <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1803-4. The famine in <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1793-94 was caused by the absence of the early fall of rain, the result being that there was no Khurreef crops at all. Latterly rain fell, and Rubbee crops came on well, which helped to make <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-97" data-page="97" data-folio="85" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n96_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 85">[p. 85]</button> the grain cheap. The second famine in <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1803-4 was caused by the army of Holkur, who looted granaries and destroyed standing crops. Grain was sold at 3 seers for the Rupee. Muka Pindaree and Hiroo Pindaree, with their followers, also looted in that year, and committed devastations. The famine prevailed at its height from April to August 1804.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ The prices stood as follows :—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ In <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1793-94—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-023"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-023 · FOLIO 97</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/report-past-famines-bombay-etheridge-1868/datasets/tables/tbl-023_p0097.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/report-past-famines-bombay-etheridge-1868/datasets/tidy/tbl-023_p0097.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>“ Wheat</td><td colspan="2">4 seers for the Rupee.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>“ Bajree</td><td class="num">5</td><td>ditto.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>“ Gram</td><td class="num">4</td><td>ditto.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>“ Rice</td><td class="num">3</td><td>ditto.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>“ Jowaree</td><td class="num">6</td><td>ditto.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>“ Dall</td><td class="num">3</td><td>ditto.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>“ Naglee</td><td class="num">6</td><td>ditto.</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ In <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1804 grain was sold at times as high as ½ seer, ¾ seer, and in some months it was 3 seers and 4 seers for the Rupee.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“The above details are given from hearsay, and not from any documents.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“Many people are said to have died. The people lived on milk and vegetable. ‘Budbudeh,’ ‘Godhdeh,’ Solé, seeds from grass, Oombri, Bhokur, and other wild fruits, &amp;c., were brought and dried, and pounded and eaten.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>From the Shreegonda Mumladar.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“1. Before the British reign, that is from the year 1396 to 1408, a great famine had occurred in the Southern Division in the Carnatic country. Kirtaram Raja offered a sacrifice to the Devee, who appeared before him and said ‘Buksh, Buksh,’ <em>i. e.</em> asked food. The Raja got terrified, and answered that ‘the kingdom is yours.’ The result was that for twelve years there was no rain.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“2. In 1791, 1792 and 1794, a famine was caused by rebellion.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“3. In 1804 and 1805 another famine occurred, caused by the army of Holkur rebelling.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“The information in paragraph 1 is not furnished from documents, but from tradition. It is said also that the prices were as follows. From 1396 to 1408 grain was sold 1 seer for the Rupee. In 1791 and 1792 the price was 2 pailees for the Rupee according to the Poona seer. In 1804 and 1805 grain was sold at 2 seers at first, and subsequently 10 seers. During the famine which lasted from 1396 to 1408 so many people are said to have died that for 80 miles together the villages were deserted. In 1791, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-98" data-page="98" data-folio="86" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n97_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 86">[p. 86]</button> 1792 and 1794, many people are said to have died from famine, and fr<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">om</span> cholera also in 1794. In 1804 also many people died. No arrangeme<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">nts</span> were made for affording relief.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“In 1845 there was a famine on account of want of rain, when gr<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">ain</span> was sold 10 seers for the Rupee, but no people died.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“No statistics are forthcoming. The above information was obtain<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">ed</span> orally from the Deshmook and Deshpandays of the Talooka.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>From the Peint Mumladar.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“The famine occurred in 1803 owing to the Holkur’s army : it last<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">ed</span> for about three months. Naglee was sold ½ seer for about 1½ months, a<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">nd</span> 1 seer subsequently. Some people went away to the port, and others to t<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">he</span> Goozerat provinces. Those who remained subsisted on ‘Bud-bu<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">l</span> ‘Koordoo,’ also on cows, buffaloes, and other animals. Some who cou<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">ld</span> not get these, roasted the flesh of men and ate. By degrees after two <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">or</span> three years everything got cheap, and people returned and established then<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">- selves</span> in the villages. No measures of relief were taken. Numberless peop<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">le</span> are said to have died. There is no documentary proof. The above infc<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">r</span>-mation has been obtained from hearsay.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“The second famine occurred in 1824 owing to the absence of rain. <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">It</span> lasted for five or six months. Latterly rain fell, and everything began <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">to</span> prosper. During this famine Naglee was sold at 8 seers for the Rupee. <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">A</span> little more than 1,200 men may have died in this State during famin<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">e.</span> People subsisted on roots, and others imported grain from above the Ghau<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">ts.</span> No measures of relief were afforded. No documentary evidence is fort<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">h</span>-coming.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“The third famine occurred in 1845: it lasted for six months : it was tl<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">ie</span> result of absence of rain. Grain was sold at 3 pailees, <em>i. e.</em> 12 seers. The<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">re</span> is no documentary evidence. About 1,000 men may have died. No me<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">a</span>-sures for affording relief were adopted.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“The fourth famine occurred in 1854 owing to the absence of rai<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">n.</span> Above 500 men may have died for want of food. Grain was sold at <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">4</span> pailees. It was imported from above the Ghauts. No documentary proo<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">f</span> are forthcoming. Such papers are usually burnt when huts, &amp;c., take fire</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>From Meer Nuzzur Ali, wulud Moonshee Meer Ghassi, Jagheerdar, reside<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">nt</span> of Ahmednuggur.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“My grandfather, named Syed Meher Ali, who was Moonshee in th<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">e</span> Ahmednuggur Collector’s Office, has left a manuscript volume written b<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">y</span> him, and entitled ‘Turieek Meherr,’ in which he has noted the importan<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">t</span> occurrences in the Collectorate. The year in which this pamphlet wa<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">s</span> written cannot be ascertained. Some information regarding famines is <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">to</span> be found in it, which I beg to transcribe below.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-99"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="99" data-folio="87" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n98_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 87">[p. 87]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“A famine occurred in <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1790, when grain was sold at 3 or 4 seers for the Rupee.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“A great famine occurred about <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1803, when all kinds of grain were sold at one seer for the Rupee. Many people who had even ample money suffered from starvation, and many died for want of food. Some cooked and ate their children. During this famine grain was forthcoming within the fort outside the city, and it was decided by the ‘Killedar’ that people should throw earth round the fort ditch, and all who did so were daily allowed ½ seer of grain each.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“In 1824 grain was sold at 2 pailees for the Rupee.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“In 1845 grain was sold at 2 and 2½ pailees for the Rupee.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">44. Mr. Oliphant, the Acting Collector of Poona, forwarded the following reports of his Assistants on 11th December last—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>From the First Assistant, Mr. Bell, dated 23rd September.</em></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“The first instance which has been brought to my notice of suffering arising from either famine or a scarcity of grain in the Poona districts was in <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1792-93, when no rain fell till October, and consequently all Khureef crops failed. The price of grain rose to 4 seers for the Rupee, and maintained that position for four months in the markets near Poona, and for twelve months in the Mawul and Joonere districts. The rain-fall in October enabled the Ryots to obtain Rubbee crops, but this was not enough to alleviate the prevailing distress, so the Peishwa’s Government brought grain from the Nizam’s country, and distributed it gratis to the sufferers There is no special mention made of a scarcity of water, nor does it appear that the scarcity of grain was so great as to deserve the name of a famine. But few people left the country, or died from want.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“2. The next instance arose from the invasion of the Deccan by Jeshwuntrao Holkur in <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1802, and the consequent devastation of the country. Rain fell favourably enough for the crops, but owing to the political state of the country fields were left waste, or where.cultivated were destroyed or reaped by the contending forces. What made matters worse was that people from other districts, perhaps more severely devasted, crowded into these districts for protection and sustenance. The result was that for twelve months the price of grain stood at one seer for the Rupee. The Peishwa’s Government did what it could to relieve the starving by bringing grain from other countries and distributing it, and by remitting the land rent. Notwithstanding this, many died of starvation, and cholera superinduced by want, and many left the country in the hope of obtaining sustenance elsewhere. Again in <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1824-25 no rain fell till October, and the Khureef crops consequently failed. It was a year very similar to that <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-100" data-page="100" data-folio="88" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n99_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 88">[p. 88]</button> of <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1792-93, though the price of grain varied only from 6 to 10 seers the Rupee during the worst months, and the difficulty of obtaining grain was considerably less. Government did not consider it necessary to take any steps to relieve those in want beyond being liberal in granting remissions of rent. That year, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1824-25, was one of scarcity only as compared with preceding and succeeding years. Compare it with the past five years during which the price of bajree has varied from 7 to 12 seers the Rupee, and it will be found that the present years press as heavily on a large class of the recipients of fixed incomes as that exceptional year pressed on the people of this district.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>From the Second Assistant, Mr. East, dated 3rd October.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“1. As directed in your No. 2253, of July 9th, 1867, I have the honor to forward what information I have been able to collect with reference to previous famines and seasons of scarcity in the districts under my charge. I would observe, however, that, except in one instance, documentary evidence, dating back before the commencement of British rule, has not been attainable, and since that period, although seasons of scarcity have not been unfrequent, no famines, properly so called, are on record. Persons who were questioned as likely to be able to produce ancient manuscripts bearing on the subject stated that any they possessed had been given over to the Inam Commission, in which case they will be readily accessible to Major Etheridge.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“2. The earliest famine of which any mention is made is stated to have occurred in the year <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1200, and to have lasted for twelve years, during which time great numbers of people died. Measures of relief are said to have been adopted by the Mogul Government, but owing to the lapse of time their precise nature cannot be ascertained.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“3. Another famine, but of short duration, occurred in the year <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1792, grain selling at 4 to 5 seers the Rupee: it is not, however, stated that any persons actually died of hunger. In consequence of the disturbed state of the country between the years <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1798 and <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1805, a large quantity of land was allowed to lie fallow. Towards the end of that time the crops were seriously damaged by heavy falls of rain and hail, the result being a great scarcity of grain, which sold at 1¼ seers the Rupee. Many of the poorer classes died of hunger, but the bulk of the ryots were at that time well to do, and enabled by the sale of their effects to keep themselves from starvation. The poorer classes were assisted to a certain extent by private charity, but nothing seems to have been done by the Government.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“4. Since the commencement of British rule there have occurred, as I stated before, no actual famines. The year <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1824-25 was one of <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-101" data-page="101" data-folio="89" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n100_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 89">[p. 89]</button> comparative scarcity, grain selling at 10 to 12 seers the Rupee, a high price for those times, although now it would be considered cheap. Again, the year <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1845-46 was a year of scarcity. In the Indapoor Talooka about Rs. 60,000 of revenue was remitted. Since then till the year <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1862-63 the seasons appear to have been fairly good; but from that time till the present they have been so bad that but for the smallness of the area affected, and the improved means of communication, a famine as severe as those of <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">for</span>mer days would probably have been the result. In the year <span class="sc">a. d.</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">186</span>6-67 more than Rs. 80,000 were remitted in the Talookas of Bheemthurry and Indapoor, and upwards of Rs. 60,000 left over for collection next year. In addition, Government sanctioned a sum of Rs. 20,000 to be spent in giving employment to the poorer classes, which has been the means of alleviating to a very great extent the prevailing distress. The prospects of the present season are, I regret to say, little, if any, better than they have been for the last four years."</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">45. These very scanty reports were supplemented by Mr. Lloyd, Agent for Sirdars in the Deccan, with the following letters from some of the Chiefs:—</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>From Raja Ruttonsing Jadhowrao, Maligaonkur.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"A letter dated 1st July 1867 from Major Etheridge, Compiler of the Presidency Famine Report, having been received, you requested me in your communication No. 182, dated 22nd idem, to collect and furnish from old records, family dufturs, or from other papers in my possession, such information as might be procurable on the subject of famines and scarcities during the late and present Governments, together with the measures of relief which were then adopted, forwarding at the same time any documents throwing light on the subject. In reply to this letter I beg to state as follows.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"1. The following are the years during which failures of rain and famines occurred:—</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><ul class="editorial-list"><li>“ I—Shuke 1709, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1787.</li><li>“ II—Shuke 1715, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1793.</li><li>“ III—Shuke 1726, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1804—In this year a seer of grain was sold for a Rupee.</li><li>“ IV—Shuke 1746, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1824—In this year 2½ pailees of grain were sold for a Rupee.</li><li>“ V—Shuke 1754, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1832—In this year want of rain caused the failure of crops.</li></ul></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-102"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="102" data-folio="90" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n101_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 90">[p. 90]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ 2. The extent of the distress and its results :—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ I—The Ryots having been subjected to many hardships in consequence of the incursions of Scindia and Holkur, at last fled the country, and left the soil uncultivated. There were many people who were rich enough to purchase grain however dear, but it was so scarce that it could not be had for money. This occurred in Shuke 1726, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1804.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ II—In course of time the Peishwa's Government arranged for a supply of grain by importation from other countries, and abolished tolls that it might be imported free. The Peishwa also granted remissions, cowls and Tuccavees, to the Ryots. By such measures as these the people where induced to return and re-commence the cultivation of the land.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ 3. I cannot say how far beyond the Poona districts this distress extended.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ 4. The measures adopted for relief of such distress were usually as follow—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ I—Brahmins were employed by the Peishwa's Government to perform certain ceremonies to propitiate the god of rain. Charities and alms were distributed. Ceremonial worship was continued to be performed even for some time after the accession of the present rule.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ II—Whenever want of rain caused famines the Peishwa's Government granted remissions of revenue, advances and cowls, and thus protected the Ryots.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ III—The Peishwa and his subordinate Sirdars very often employed villagers in the different departments of the State; sometimes in the Cavalry, Infantry, Elephant, and Camel branches of the service. For these duties they received remuneration. By this means the Ryot was both enabled to pay the assessment on the field which he cultivated and to find subsistence on the balance; so that he did not always stand in need of support from Government in times of scarcity.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ The above information I give from memory and the tradition handed down from my ancestors. No old documents of a nature to furnish information on the subject are forthcoming.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>From Rughoonathrao Vittul Vinchoorkur.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ In reply to your letter No. 181, dated 22nd July 1867, desiring me to furnish from old Dufturs, family records, or other papers, information on <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-103" data-page="103" data-folio="91" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n102_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 91">[p. 91]</button> the subject of past famines and scarcities in the Bombay Presidency, I beg to state as follows :—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ I—In Shuke 1725, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1802, the Pindarees having wasted the country during a period of about three months, viz., from July to September, grain was sold as dear as ½ a seer a Rupee, though there was no lack of rain.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ II—The poor found it very hard to maintain themselves, but as abundance of rain had fallen there was no want of vegetables. Such as could eat flesh ate flesh. Many emigrated to the Concan and Goozerat. In the latter country there were guards posted at different places by the Guicowar, so the emigrants found it even difficult to procure the common jungle fruits to subsist upon. When the Guicowar heard this he removed the guards, and allowed the emigrants to gather the fruits growing in the jungles. About the same time the Sowkars in Bombay, and the widow of Nana Fudnavis, opened free mess-houses in Bombay. The severity of the famine just before its termination was so great that mothers are said to have eaten their own children. Afterwards ‘ Tumans’ and ‘ Charuns’ imported grain into the country and relieved the sufferings of the poor.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ III—The territory extending from the Nurbudda to Pundhurpoor was for the most part affected by the distress.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ IV—Neither Government nor the public adopted any special measures of relief on this occasion. The strong oppressed and looted the weak with impunity.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ The above is the only information that I have been able to gather. The old records of the family, which were deposited at the Fort of Ankaee, were destroyed on the occasion of the Government taking it. Some papers which were at Vinchoor were destroyed by fire along with the house which was burnt by Holkur.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>From Krishnarao Nilkunt Moozumdar.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ A letter dated 1st July 1867 from Major Etheridge, Compiler of the Presidency Famine Report, having been received, you requested me in your letter No. 188, dated 22nd idem, to collect and furnish from old Dufturs, family records, or other papers in my possession, or from private sources, such information as might be procurable respecting past famines and scarcities, also the measures of relief which were then adopted, forwarding, at the same time, any documents throwing light on the subject. In reply, I beg to state as follows.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ 1. The following are the years during which failures of rain and famines occurred :—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ I—Shuke 1709, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1787. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-104" data-page="104" data-folio="92" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n103_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 92">[p. 92]</button> “ II—Shuke 1715, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1793.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ III—Shuke 1726, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1804—In this year a seer of grain was sold for a Rupee.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ IV—Shuke 1746, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1824—In this year 2½ pailees of grain were sold for a Rupee.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ V—Shuke 1754, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1832—In this year want of rain caused the failure of crops, and in some instances the Ryots plundered shops.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">“ 2. The extent of the distress and its results.</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ I—The disturbances caused by the invasion of Scindia and Holkur, and the hardships which followed thereupon to the Ryots, made them leave the country, and so the soil remained uncultivated. Grain was so scarce that it could not be had for money even by rich people. This occurred in Shuke 1726, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1804.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ II—Subsequently the Peishwa's Government arranged for supplying the Ryots with grain from other countries, abolished tolls in order to its free importation, and granted remissions of revenue, cowls and advances. By these means people were induced to return and undertake the cultivation of the land.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ 3. It is not known how far the distress extended. So much is known that it affected the Poona districts.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">“ 4. The measures adopted for relief were as follow :—</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ I—The Peishwa's Government employed Brahmins to perform propitiatory worship to the god of rain, and distributed charities and alms. These ceremonies were continued even for some years after the introduction of the present rule.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ II—Whenever want of rain caused distress, the Peishwa's Government granted remissions, advances and cowls, and thus protected the Ryots.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ I write this from memory and family tradition. No old documents of a nature to furnish information are in my possession.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>From Rungrao Venayeh, Poorundhuray.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ I received your letter No. 185, dated 22nd July 1867, in which, on a letter dated 1st idem from Major Etheridge, Compiler of the Presidency Famine Report, you desire me to furnish information respecting the times and extents of past famines and scarcities; in reply to which I beg to state as follows :—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ I—There was a great famine in Shuke 1724, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1802, which is known as the year of disturbance of Holkur. The second famine <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-105" data-page="105" data-folio="93" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n104_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 93">[p. 93]</button> occcurred in Fuslee 1234, <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1824, which is called the year of the ‘Khurpud.’ And the third famine has continued from Fuslee 1272, <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1862, up to the present time, and is styled the current dearness of grain.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“<em>A.</em>—The first famine, that of Shuke 1724, <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1802, resulted from the invasion of Holkur. There was then such a scarcity of grain that it was not procurable for money. Thousands of people died of starvation. The consequence was that the district bore the aspect of a desert.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“<em>B.</em>—The second famine, that of Shuke 1746, <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1824, the year of the ‘Khurpud,’ arose from a general failure of rain. Grain, though old and rotten, was sold at 10 seers a Rupee. Epidemics broke out. There was universal sickness. Some people fled the country. Cattle died from want of grass.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“<em>C.</em>—The third famine, the present dearness of grain, is not so severe as those above mentioned. It owes its origin partly to the failure of rain, and partly to the taxes imposed on communities who are barely able to support themselves.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“II—The first famine affected almost the whole of the Deccan. The second, that of Shuke 1746, <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1824, the districts of Poona, Nuggur, Candeish, and Sholapoor. The third still prevails in all parts of India.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“III—The distress occasioned by the first famine was mitigated by the importation of grain from other countries. Government also reduced the assessment on land, and encouraged and assisted traders to import grain freely.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“IV—The distress caused by the second famine, that of Shuke 1746, <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1824, was lessened by an opportune fall of rain on the day of ‘Dusera,’ and good crops in some districts. Subsequently harvests became more flourishing.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“V—The distress caused by the third famine is still prevailing. It rains not as required. Servants, labourers, &amp;c., have to pay very high prices for the necessaries of life.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“The above information is stated from what I have heard and gathered from other people. No documentary evidence of a nature to throw light on the subject is with me.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>From Munohur Bheorao, Khasnavis and Potnis.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“In reply to your letters Nos. 183 and 214, dated 24th August 1867, desiring me to collect and furnish from old records, family dufturs and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-106" data-page="106" data-folio="94" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n105_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 94">[p. 94]</button> other papers in my possession, or from other sources, information on the subject of the famines and scarcities which have occurred during the late and present Governments, the extent of the distress occasioned, the area of the country affected, and the measures of relief adopted, I beg to state that no documentary evidence of a nature to throw light on this subject is in my possession, but from hearsay and family tradition I am able to say as follows:—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“I—In Shuke 1714, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1792, a famine occurred, during which dearness of grain prevailed to such an extent that a pailee of grain was sold for a Rupee in the Poona districts. In Shuke 1726, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1804, a second famine occurred, during which one seer of grain was sold for a Rupee. These two famines resulted from failure of rain.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“II—The distress extended over the southern part of the Poona districts.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“III—For the relief of the distress the Peishwa’s Government employed Brahmins to perform the usual ceremonies to propitiate the Almighty to send down rain, distributed charities and alms, established free mess-houses, granted advances and cowls, and, after inspection of crops, allowed remission of assessment. Tolls were also abolished in order to the free importation of grain, and traders were encouraged to bring it from other countries. By these means the Ryots were protected.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“IV—After the introduction of the British rule, owing to the failure of rain in Shuke 1746, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1824, grain was sold from 2 to 2½ pailees the Rupee. This distress continued up to Ashween Shood 10th, on which day rain fell and produced good crops. In Shuke 1754, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1832, the grain-sellers raised the price of grain, but Government issued orders and stopped the evil.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“The above is all the information I have been able to collect.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>From Nilkuntrao Mahadeo Poorundhuray.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“In reply to your letter No. 187, dated 2nd July 1867, desiring me to furnish from old dufturs, family records, or other papers in my possession, or from private sources, information respecting past famines and scarcities, together with the measures of relief adopted, forwarding at the same time any documents throwing light on the subject, I regret to state that I am not in a position to comply, having in my possession no documentary evidence, and not deeming tradition or hearsay reliable.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>From Kesheorao Ramchundur Jog.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“In reply to your letter of 22nd July 1867, desiring me to furnish <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-107" data-page="107" data-folio="95" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n106_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 95">[p. 95]</button> information regarding famines and scarcities prior to and since the introduction of the British rule, I beg to state as follows :—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>" I—During the Peishwa’s rule, in <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1803-4, the armies of Scindia and Holkur came to Poona and fought. Plunder to a great extent was the consequence. There was also a drought, and the crops suffered. Grain was sold from a quarter of a seer to one seer for a Rupee, and even at this rate could be procured with difficulty ; consequently great numbers of people died of starvation. I know nothing regarding the measures adopted for the relief of the distress, as I was very young. I am unable to procure any documents throwing light on the subject, nor can I state precisely the extent of the country affected, nor the period during which the famine lasted.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>" II—After the introduction of the British rule a drought occurred in <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1824, and grain was not sown in sufficient quantities. It consequently became dear. To alleviate the distress Government commenced the construction of the Koorkoomb and Babdeo Ghauts, and thus gave employment to sufferers. The scarcity of grain in those days, however, was not greater than that which has prevailed during the last four years. Fodder was not so dear as it is now. It is said that the scarcity affected an area of about 100 miles round Poona.</p></blockquote></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><em>From Wamunrao Mahadeo, Raheereckur.</em></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">" I received your letter No. 186, dated 22nd July last, desiring me to furnish information regarding famines and scarcities, and in reply I beg to state as follows :—</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>During the late Rule.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>" In Fuslee 1201-2, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1791-92, grain was sold at from 6 to 7 seers for a Rupee in the districts round Poona and in the Concan. Although there was a sufficient fall of rain, and consequent good harvests, still a scarcity arose from the bringing together of the troops intended for the expedition of Khurda. When this was over the country again became prosperous. People did not suffer much from this distress.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>" In Fuslee 1213-14, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1803-4, a Rupee would buy only 1½ or 1¾ seers of grain. This dearness lasted for three or four months. It arose from the incursion of Scindia and Holkur. Throughout the districts of Khandeish, Nuggur and Poona, where it rained, and there were consequent good crops, they were laid waste by the armies of those Chiefs. Other districts were in a flourishing state. When the disturbance ceased the prices of grain began to decline. For the relief of this distress Government granted remissions of revenue, and abolished the customs. Rich people <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-108" data-page="108" data-folio="96" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n107_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 96">[p. 96]</button> brought grain from other countries, and supplied the poor in charity. Government also employed Brahmins to propitiate the deity to send down rain and bless the sufferers.</p></blockquote></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>During the present Rule.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“Fuslee 1233-4, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1823-24, in which year there was a famine, was known as the year of the ‘Khurpud.’ It rained at the commencement of the monsoon, but no rain fell after. On the day of ‘Dusera’ much rain fell, and Ryots who had grain at once sowed it, but no crops resulted. The price of grain rose very high. This distress lasted for the whole year. Government granted Ryots lands in farm, some free from assessment and some on Mamool assessment, and relieved them from distress in this manner.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“In Fuslee 1242-3, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1832-33, there was a famine, which owed its origin to failure of rain, and high prices resulted.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“Since Fuslee 1273, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1863, up to the present time the price of grain has continued high. People earn their livelihood with great difficulty. Government partially relieved this distress by granting to their servants compensation on account of high prices, and by sanctioning works of public utility in the Engineering and Railway Departments. Cultivators are enabled to support themselves, and pay Government with ease the light assessment on their fields, from the produce of their crops in good seasons. Moreover, Government have imposed a new tax, and as the poor are unable to work this tax acts as a bar to trade. The Sowkars have become bankrupt. These causes continue to make people pass their days with difficulty.”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">46. Mr. Arthur, the Collector of Sattara, gives very little information, and regrets that “the entire absence of records makes it impossible to supply more.” That gentleman writes on 17th January last as follows:—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“1. With reference to your Circular of the 1st July last, I have the honor to inform you that no information can be obtained in this district regarding any famines which may have occurred in years beyond the memory of men now living. All the old papers in the possession of families of note in this part of the country were taken away by the Officers of the Inam Commission, and are for the most part, I believe, recorded in your Office.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“2. The famine years regarding which oral information is forthcoming are <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1792, 1803 and 1824.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“3. In the first of these years the famine appears to have raged throughout the Sattara territory. It was occasioned partly by the scanty <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-109" data-page="109" data-folio="97" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n108_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 97">[p. 97]</button> fall of rain, and partly by the political troubles which then prevailed. It lasted, it seems, about a year, during which time grain was sold for the most part at 3 seers per Rupee.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“4. The Peishwa’s Government is said to have then endeavoured, as far as possible, to relieve the distress which was so generally felt throughout the Deccan, but I cannot find that any public works of importance in the Sattara territory had their origin in his liberality.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“5. The dearth of <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1803 does not appear to have had its origin in a failure of crops in this part of the country: in the Nizam’s country, and also in Goozerat, there appears to have been a great failure of crops, owing to which people flocked in great numbers from distant parts to Sattara, and the result was to create a famine similar to that which prevailed elsewhere. This famine is said to have reached its height in the months of July and August, and stories up to this time are told, with horror, of men having been reduced to the extremity of subsisting on human flesh. Corn is said to have been sold at one seer per Rupee, and so many as 25,000 strangers are said to have flocked into the town of Waee to obtain what they could from the liberality of the Punt Pritinidhi, the Rastia, and such like wealthy persons. It is stated that no fewer than 10,000 persons died in Waee during this famine.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“6. The dearth of <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1824 does not appear to have been so severe, and perhaps was more local in its nature. Grain was then sold, it is said, at about 6 seers for a Rupee, and people emigrated for food both in the direction of Nuggur and of Kolapoor.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“7. I regret that I can only give this general account regarding what has occurred in this district, but the entire absence of records makes it impossible to supply more detailed information.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">47. From Sholapoor the Sub-Collector, Mr. G. W. Anderson, reported on 6th September that there were no official records which gave any account of past famines, and that he could do no more than forward what information the Mumludars had been able to obtain from private sources. The several Mumludars and the District Deputy Collector wrote as follows:—</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>Sholapoor Mamludar.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ Since the introduction of the British rule, in Fuslee 1227, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1817, a great famine, called ‘Kurdee famine,’ occurred in Fuslee year 1228, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1818, when people lived by eating ‘Kurdee.’ Two famines afterwards raged in Fuslee years 1241-42 and 1264-65, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1831-32 and 1854-55, owing to want of rain, and they extended over the whole of the Talooka. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-110" data-page="110" data-folio="98" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n109_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 98">[p. 98]</button> It does not appear that Government afforded any other means of relief to the famine-stricken people except by employing them on works of public utility. The merchants also lent their aid to the distressed by distributing grain and food to them. Before the commencement of the British rule the rate of grain was 1¼ seer for the Rupee, and the Peishwa's Government distributed food to the sufferers.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>Barsee Mamludar.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“The great Doorga Deevee famine took place in <span class="sc">A.
