Mr. Elphinston, the Acting Collector of North Canara, reported on 14th December as follows :—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“1. In reply to your Circular dated the 1st of July, I have the honor to report that, though North Canara has on different occasions suffered from a scarcity, the only one which, from its duration, extent, and the area of country affected, can be considered to have been a famine was in the year <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1806, or the Ksheya Sumwatsara. “2. The old records of this Zilla are still at Mangalore, but reports show this famine to have been very severe : men were reduced to feed on <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-135" data-page="135" data-folio="123" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n134_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 123">[p. 123]</button> roots in the jungle and on husks of rice: about 3,000 persons are said to have died of starvation and want. The famine was not confined to this district, but extended to Rutnagherry, Malwan, and Punderpoor. Indeed, the scarcity here first began by an immense influx of people from those districts, and was augmented by the want of good roads for bringing rice from the below to the above Ghaut district by the depredations of dacoities, and by the practice, common in those days, of forbidding the export of grain to famine-stricken districts. In this way it is said that exportation from Dharwar to Canara was not permitted. The distress lasted from Powesh in one year to Phalgoon in the next.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“3. The only measures which appear from the records in our possession to have been used was the prohibition of any exporting of rice from the district, and an order from the Collector directing the purchase of rice by Government and its re-sale at moderate prices to the people. It also appears that the land assessment was remitted, and that advances subsequently recovered were made to cultivators for agricultural purposes.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“4. Both this famine and the different occasions of scarcity which have followed it appears to have originated with an insufficient fall of rain. I regret that the records do not furnish more detailed information on this important subject, but the moral pointed to, by observance of which famines will become rarer in future, is the improvement of our communications and of irrigationary tanks, and of the economizing in large reservoirs of the vast quantity of water which finds its way to the sea every monsoon in our rivers and wide Nullahs, the encouragement of agriculture by a low assessment, the removal of all interference with the export of trade from where it is cheap to where it is dear, and the preservation of peace and order by a proper distribution of the Police.”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">65. The above completes the information supplied by the Collectors and Political Officers of the Presidency.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">66. In paragraph 15 I remarked that it was my intention to supplement this information by an epitome of the evidence forthcoming from the Peishwa’s Dafter relating to events prior to the introduction of the British Government, and by a Summary from the records of the Secretariat and other sources available in respect to events since.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">67. But, for the reason stated in paragraph 3, the epitome can best take the form of Appendices: they are lettered from A to G, and annexed.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-136"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="136" data-folio="124" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n135_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 124">[p. 124]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">68. The Secretariat Summary may, however, be embodied in the report.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">69. It may be well, in the first instance, to refer to the Minute dated 10th June 1805 by the Honorable Mr. Duncan, Governor of Bombay, reviewing certain irregularities on the part of Mr. Henshaw, Customs Master General, and in charge of the Grain Department temporarily formed in 1802 in Bombay, as it contains an account of the measures adopted by Government to avert or mitigate the effects of the scarcity which threatened Bombay, Poona and Surat, in that year.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">70. This Minute on the famine of 1802 in the Bombay Presidency by the Honorable Jonathan Duncan, Governor and President in Council of Bombay, is given entire. It forms Appendix H.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">71. The fall of rain in that year having been very scanty a famine was considered imminent, and prompt measures were accordingly taken to alleviate the impending distress.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">72. Government, on the suggestion of Mr. Henshaw, at once prohibited the exportation of grain from Bombay, and, in order to encourage or afford greater facilities for the importation of grain and other necessaries of life, suspended the levy of town duties. Government also authorized 200 bags of rice from their store being daily placed in the bazar for sale at fixed prices under the directions of the Police Superintendent of Bombay. Rice was also imported from Mangalore and thrown into the market for sale; and Mr. Henshaw was authorized to purchase rice from individual importers in such quantities as to him might appear necessary without causing any inconvenient drain on the General Treasury. Thus every endeavour was made to stock the market with sufficient grain and other necessaries, and to keep the Town and Island of Bombay clear of the impending famine.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">73. Scarcity of grain was about the same time felt in Poona, then the capital of the Peishwa's Government; and applications having been received from that Government, through the Resident, for permission to allow the Brinjaries and other merchants to export grain from and through Bombay, a certain proportion of the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-137" data-page="137" data-folio="125" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n136_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 125">[p. 125]</button> quantity then in store with the Grain Department was set apart for exportation to Poona. In compliance also with a request made by the Resident at Poona, Government permitted grain of different sorts, used as food by the Natives, to be imported from Goozerat and other grain-producing countries by private individuals and merchants for the Peishwa's use <em>viâ</em> Bombay, and allowed the same to pass free of duty.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">74. About the same time a famine threatened Surat, and, in compliance with urgent representations from the Authorities there, large quantities of rice were exported to that place under such arrangements as seemed to Government to be advisable.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">75. It appears from the Honorable Mr. Duncan's Minute above referred to that, under the arrangements which at the instance of the Government were adopted by Mr. Henshaw, in charge of the Grain Department, the worst effects of the distress which prevailed in Bombay, Poona and Surat, were averted. This was the happy result of the promptness with which supplies were obtained, and made available wherever most needed. There is, however, no mention made of any works of public utility having been started at this time under the authority of Government, or of any other enterprize having been undertaken, with a view of affording aid to the suffering population.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">76. In the year 1812 there was another famine. It extended further than that of 1802. It affected Goozerat; and particularly Kaira, Surat and Broach. It was felt also in Kutch, Kattyawar, Baroda and Marwar. Marwar suffered most. In Bombay the scarcity continued only for a short time, and was, perhaps, more the result of the eagerness of the merchants to send their grain to the famine-stricken districts in the hope of realizing large profits. The first indications of the distress were brought to notice by the Customs Master General at Bombay, who, as on former occasions, suggested that the export of grain and other necessaries of life should, for a time, be prohibited. Government, however, did not think it expedient to take any measures that might tend to interfere with the ordinary course of trade. They were of opinion that it would only be necessary to warn the dealers, who might have <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-138" data-page="138" data-folio="126" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n137_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 126">[p. 126]</button> hoarded their stock with a view to monopoly and large profits, that if they persevered in that course Government would be compelled to establish a control over their proceedings. So great was . the distress in Kattyawar that many of the inhabitants abandoned their homés. They went to Baroda and other parts of Goozerat where scarcity did not prevail to the same extent, while large numbers emigrated to Scinde.<sup>•</sup> The country was reduced to the most deplorable condition. It was only partially relieved by supplies from Baroda and Malwa. The fear of want also afforded pretext for the disorderly tribes of Coolies in the neighbouring provinces to commence their depredations. It then became necessary to maintain a regular force for the protection of the country against marauders. At the Military Stations of Pally and Versovah the scarcity was so keenly felt that fears were entertained of an outbreak on the part of the Sepoys. Grain was supplied to these stations from the Government Stores. In order that Government might be supplied with authentic and reliable information from time to time of the condition of the districts in which scarcity prevailed, a Committee was formed at Broach, consisting of the Collector, the Judge and Magistrate, and the Commanding Officer of the Station. The Committee was directed to prepare and forward a weekly report of the state of the grain market at Broach and its dependencies, to enable Government to adopt such measures as might seem to be called for with a view to afford aid to the starving population.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">77. A similar Committee was formed at Kaira, composed of the local Officers of the Province, for the purpose of superintending the measures which it might be necessary to adopt for relieving the distress further north. Government did not, however, consider it advisable to impose immediate prohibition on export, as suggested by the Collector of Kaira. It was thought that the scarcity in the interior of the country would necessarily operate to the entire prevention of export; but should it not, the Committee were left at liberty to prohibit export of grain from any part of the Gulf of Cambay as so on asthat step might appear to them to be necessary. It was also left to the discretion of the Committee to suspend import duties.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-139"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="139" data-folio="127" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n138_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 127">[p. 127]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">78. Government at the same time authorized advances of money (Tuccavee) to be made to enable the Ryots to bring their fields under cultivation.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">79. At the suggestion of the Collector of Kaira, Government also directed the construction of dams for irrigational purposes in the districts suffering most from want. They acceded also to another proposal made by the Collector, viz., the extension of acts of beneficence in extraordinary cases.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">80. Another Committee was at this time formed at Surat with the same view.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">81. At this conjuncture information of the very unfavorable aspect of the season and the fear entertained of a scarcity was communicated by this Government to the Government of India, who were, at the same time, requested to permit the export of supplies of grain from Bengal to Bombay for the consumption of the Honorable Company's Troops quartered in this Presidency.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">82. The Government of India gave the permission solicited, and on arrival of the ships with the grain notices were sent to the several District Officers; but Government did not think it prudent to interfere with the Consignees in the disposal of their consignments.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">83. The Judge and Magistrate of Kaira, in addressing Government on the subject of the distress prevailing in that district and in other parts of Goozerat, suggested, among other measures, the expediency of purchasing on Government account all the grain which could be procured in the country, the resale of which could be so regulated as to price as barely to cover the expenses and give a moderate profit to the retailer. Such a measure he considered to be indispensably necessary to secure a stock for the consumption of the Troops, as it would induce the production of concealed stores from the certainty of prices being reduced when the demands of Government should have ceased to influence the markets, and the surplus produce should have been sold by Government below the rates which could then be obtained. Government, after approving of the arrangements made by the Judge and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-140" data-page="140" data-folio="128" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n139_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 128">[p. 128]</button> Magistrate of Kaira for the preservation of public tranquillity, recorded the following observations in respect to the above proposal:—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"The Right Honourable the Governor in Council is disposed to think, from all the information before the Board, that those approved and recognized principles (of political economy), founded as they have been on the experience of the prejudicial effects of a contrary policy in all ages and in all countries, which prescribe an entire and unrestrained freedom in the grain trade, as best adapted to the relief of any existing scarcity and to the prevention of famine, are particularly applicable to the dealers in grain in the province of Goozerat. Whatever may be the state of society in that province, or the character of those in whom the control of the markets may exist, still it is universally admitted that the Natives of India are as provident of their own interests, and are as keen, vigilant and sagacious, in observing and benefiting by the course of events, and are as conversant in those principles of traffic which point out the periods when their gains will be the greatest and their losses the most severe, as any other description of men in any other country, and that, therefore, any interposition on the part of the ruling authority in their commercial concerns or speculations, or any attempts to ascertain their hidden stores of grain, are more calculated to accelerate the dreadful crisis of a famine than to produce any salutary relief to the exigencies of the public.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"The digression of the celebrated Author of the Wealth of Nations concerning the Corn Trade and Corn Laws, and particularly as far as respects the <em>inland dealer</em>, is forcibly and irresistibly applicable to every state of society where merchants, or dealers in grain, may be established. It is observed by that Author that 'in the history of the dearths and famines which have afflicted any part of Europe it will be found that a dearth has never arisen from any combination among the inland dealers in corn, nor from any other cause but a real scarcity occasioned in most instances by the fault of the seasons, and that a famine has never arisen from any other cause but the violence of Government attempting, by improper means, to remedy the inconveniences of a dearth;' and again that 'even in rice countries the drought is, perhaps, scarce ever so universal as necessarily to occasion a famine if the Government would allow a free trade; that the drought in Bengal a few years ago (that is prior to 1793) might probably have occasioned a very great dearth; some improper regulations, some injudicious restraints imposed by the servants of the East India Company upon the rice trade, contributed perhaps to turn that dearth into a famine.'</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"If an interference on the part of Government is likely to accelerate a dearth into a famine at a time of scarcity, how much more objectionable <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-141" data-page="141" data-folio="129" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n140_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 129">[p. 129]</button> would it be to adopt measures of coercion against the grain dealers at a time when it is known that there is a presumable supply in the country, as far as Mr. Keate is enabled to judge, equal to twelve months' consumption.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“But, whatever may be the actual state of things in the neighbourhood of Kaira, apprehensions must be entertained that any proceedings on the part of Government with a view of compelling a discovery of the supplies in the possession of the dealers, or to impose any restraints upon their trade, would create alarms in individuals for the preservation of their property that will lead them the more effectually to secrete their stock of grain, and thus prematurely hasten the existing dearth, as was experienced in Bengal, to a famine; or, in the words of the author already quoted, ‘when the Government, in order to remedy the incoveniences of a dearth, orders all dealers to sell their corn at what it supposes a reasonable price, it either hinders them from bringing it to market, which may sometimes produce a famine even in the beginning of the season; or if it bring it thither it enables the people, and thereby encourages them, to consume it so fast as must necessarily produce a famine before the end of the season;’ nor does a measure of this description seem politic or advisable when the fair season has opened, and imports into Goozerat may be daily expected; in addition to which the abundant supplies in Malwa and Scinde, and the promising harvests in the Broach and Surat jurisdiction, are better calculated to operate as a beneficial influence on the grain dealers of Goozerat, in encouraging them to bring their grain for sale into the market before the prices fall than any measure which the Government can adopt.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“Under the influence of these impressions, the Right Honorable the Governor in Council would have felt disposed to have persevered in the resolution, already formed, of not controlling the markets beyond the provision of a supply for our own troops, even were the prospects of a famine more certain and alarming than they appear to be; at a time, however, when the accounts before Government represent that the stock in the country is equal to twelve months' consumption; when the supplies in Malwa, Scinde and Candeish, are stated to be abundant; when the harvests at Broach and Surat are luxuriant and promising, and are even in Goozerat not yet hopeless; at a time, also, when the merchants and grain dealers in Bombay have, under an assurance that the Government will not impose any prohibitions in the grain trade, largely embarked in speculations for the relief of the provinces of Kattyawar and Goozerat, it would, under such circumstances, be a measure not only of extreme hazard to a mitigation of the immediate exigencies of the dearth, but also of irremediable prejudice to the future supplies of the country on the recurrence of a similar calamity, were Government, by departing from the pledge it has promulgated of the line of policy that it has judged advisable to pursue, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-142" data-page="142" data-folio="130" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n141_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 130">[p. 130]</button> to afford a proof of the little confidence that is to be reposed in its measures or character, an interference on the part of Government for the supply of a whole province would inevitably lead to a combination in the inland grain dealers and the great mercantile importers to counteract the efforts of Government, however beneficent may be its views, and would hasten the dreadful crisis of a famine; besides which it cannot be expected in any Government that they shall engage in purchases of grain for the relief of a numerous population at a period of scarcity; the public expenditure would know no bounds in such an application of its bounty, whilst the relief would be but partial and temporary, compared with the effects of that line of policy which the wisdom of experience has sanctioned as the best adapted to supply, through the medium of foreign imports and the enterprize of individual speculations, the inconveniences and pressure of a dearth in the country. It becomes the duty of a Government at a period of scarcity to extend its fostering protection by providing supplies for the relief of its own troops and establishments, and to afford to its subjects an increased degree of protection against those attacks and depredations which may be expected at such a crisis from gangs of freebooters that infest the country; and to the grain dealers and merchants every facility and security in the free and unrestrained prosecution of those speculations which can alone afford a substantial and permanent relief to an extensive population.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"The danger to be apprehended from the piratical States bordering in the Gulf of Cutch, as adverted to in the concluding paragraph, has attracted the attention of Government, and measures have been adopted for the protection of the grain boats sailing between this and the Northern Ports."</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">84. The troops stationed in the remote parts of Goozerat having suffered considerable inconvenience from the sudden rise in the price of grain at a season of the year when it was difficult to forward to them supplies from the Presidency, Government had under their consideration the expediency of establishing a Depot of grain in order to secure the soldiery against those serious embarrassments to which the troops had been exposed. This measure, it was thought, could be effected with very little or no expense to the Honorable Company by annually disposing of and renewing the stock of grain at the most favourable periods of the year. But it does not appear whether any measures were taken to carry out the formation of a depôt as contemplated.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">85. The long withheld rain appears to have favoured some of the grain-producing districts in Kattyawar and Goozerat, and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-143" data-page="143" data-folio="131" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n142_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 131">[p. 131]</button> though it was but scanty it proved favourable to some of the crops of the season. This, added to the encouragement afforded for the importation of grain into countries where the scarcity prevailed by the suspension of Import duties, by the abolition of restraints, and the arrival also of supplies from Bengal, seems to have successfully relieved the sufferings to which the Ryots were subjected in consequence of the continued drought.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">86. In the year 1824 there was again a drought of long continuance, particularly in the Deccan and in Western Goozerat, and serious apprehensions were entertained of a scarcity, or even famine. The subject early occupied the serious attention of Government, and no time was lost in adopting precautions with a view to avert, as far as practicable, the impending calamity. The following transcript of a minute by the Honorable Mountstuart Elphinstone, the late Governor and President in Council, which was concurred in by the other Members of the Honorable Board, contains an account of the proceedings which it was thought necessary to adopt in connection with this subject:—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“The Collectors should everywhere be called on ˙to state without delay the prospect they conceive there is of scarcity in their district, and the period when, if at all, it will begin to be severely felt. They should as soon as possible (at the same time with the first information, if it can be done without occasioning delay) report on the present price of grain in their district, and whether it is rising or falling; the average price in ordinary years; the quantity of grain supposed to be in the country; the countries from whence they usually draw grain when there is a scarcity in the Company’s territories; the present state of those countries and of others in the neighbourhood of their districts. They should also suggest any means by which the importation of grain from those and all other countries could be encouraged.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“Similar enquiry should be made in Bombay, and the Bench of Justices would probably be the best authority to refer to; and perhaps it would be prudent to address also the Residents at Hydrabad, Indore and Mysore, and the Collectors of Canara and the Ceded Districts, to know the state and prospects of their divisions. The Madras Government might afford similar information, as well as acquaint us with the steps which it has pursued during the late or present scarcity.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“I do not think any measures should be taken till some answers at least are received, as a fall of rain enough to prevent a famine is by no <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-144" data-page="144" data-folio="132" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n143_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 132">[p. 132]</button> means hopeless; but when we again consider the subject the unusual state of the shipping of India should not escape our attention. Many of the merchant vessels which we might trust to for bringing round grain from Bengal are now employed on the Expedition, and the high price of freight in all quarters will render it necessary to hold out great encouragement to the importation of grain. I see no way in which this can be done, except by promising a certain bounty for every ton of grain imported from countries beyond certain limits. It will, probably, be in vain to trust to the rise of price as a sufficient encouragement to importation, for in the greater part of the countries where scarcity will be severe there are no funds to bring into the market so as to raise the price to any thing like the degree required to influence trade in the present scarcity of freight.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“Some means should, therefore, be taken to ascertain the premium which will be necessary to induce importation, or the price which it will be necessary to guarantee the importers. The amount lately offered by the Madras Government, which must have appeared in the Newspapers, will be some guide, and perhaps the Secretary might, in communication with the Superintendent of Marine, obtain some information without instituting an official enquiry.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“The attention of the Commissary General might be directed to the state of the markets and to the expediency of early importing grain for our troops, cavalry horses, and public cattle. He should bring them from countries beyond the reach of the expected scarcity.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“The state of prices in Bombay and the other districts of this Presidency should be given every possible publicity, as was formerly done in 1812.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“The Collectors in Goozerat should be authorized (if they find it necessary) to dispense with the duties on grain, and perhaps with duties on the return bullock loads of Brinjarics who import grain; and the Commissioner in the Deccan might receive powers to use his discretion in all similar means of encouragement to importation.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“The Collector of Customs might, perhaps, be able to give information about the exports from this place to grain countries, and the possibility of affording encouragement by remitting the duties on return cargoes.”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">87. The necessary instructions were at once issued to all the Collectors and Magistrates, the Collector of Sea Customs and the other Officers named, and the Clerk to the Bench of Justices, as also to the Collectors of Canara and Mangalore. The Residents at Indore, Hydrabad and Mysore, were likewise addressed on the subject.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-145"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="145" data-folio="133" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n144_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 133">[p. 133]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">88. With reference to the point mooted in the last quoted paragraph of the above minute, the Collector of Sea Customs intimated that the only places beyond the territories belonging to this Presidency from which grain could be imported in large quantities were Calcutta, Malabar and Canara, chiefly Mangalore; the return cargoes to the first named place being chiefly pepper, piece goods, copper and other British staples, and to the other two places broad cloth, copper, cotton, iron, nankeen, piece goods, sugar, &amp;c. The exporters of these commodities carried certificates with them from the Customs Department of duties levied and retained in the Bombay Custom House, for which an abatement was allowed at the place of importation, so that exempting the return cargoes from paying merely the difference in Malabar, Canara and Calcutta, might probably be viewed as but a small indulgence; and although it might have some, it would not have, the Customs Collector thought, the desired effect. The great object of exporting to Calcutta was a favorable remittance, and while the exchange for the best bills was as low as 3 per cent., with no prospect of speedy rise, the export of goods from Bombay was not thought likely to increase. He therefore proposed, in imitation of the measures adopted by the Madras Government on the occasion of a scarcity in that Presidency at the commencement of that year, that a bounty should be offered on the importation of grain. The Bombay Government also contemplated suggesting to the Government of India, with a view to facilitate to shippers of grain in Calcutta the remittance of funds realized by the sale of rice, &amp;c., at Bombay, that bills should be granted here at the exchange of 105 Bombay for 100 Sicca Rupees, payable three months after date, or to run for a longer period at the option of the Supreme Government, the rate then current in Bengal being allowed for such protracted payment. The Accountant General, to whom the question was in the first instance referred for opinion, did not consider that the measure would be productive of good, while he thought it was open to objection. He pointed out that where the object was to encourage the accumulation of any particular article a direct bounty on its importation, or a remission of Customs, &amp;c., was the simplest and best means of attaining it. The Bench of Justices also recommended, among other measures, the grant of a bounty <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-146" data-page="146" data-folio="134" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n145_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 134">[p. 134]</button> on importation. At first this proposition, coming from so many Officers, and being one which was stated to have been adopted at Madras on the occasion of a scarcity there, was favourably received by Government; but, on further consideration, the idea of offering a bounty appears to have been given up as not likely to produce the desired effect. The following is a transcript of a minute recorded by one of the Members of the Government of the day on the subject :—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“ I have not the slightest confidence in the efficacy of the proposed bounty, and am persuaded that it will not lead to the importation of a single bag, or a single robin, of rice beyond what we shall receive from the powerful encouragement naturally arising out of a scarcity price. The bounty will neither create a more abundant supply nor lower the price to the consumer, but merely go into the coffers of those who stand the least in need of the money, and whose interest to throw in supplies does not appear to me to require a stimulus by the proposed bounty. It has not, I believe, been hitherto had recourse to under this Presidency, and we shall, in my opinion, do better without it. On a lac of bags the bounty will amount to Rupees one lac. How much more beneficially could this lac be appropriated by following the Madras plan, by which 20,000 souls could be kept alive for five months at a Rupee a month. But should the Board determine on the grant of the bounty, the obligation to refund it on the export to foreign country would defeat the efforts of it. Our policy should be not only to relieve ourselves, but our neighbours also, and thus to prevent their crowding into the British territories, and this the grain dealers will do if left to themselves.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“ I have every reliance in the grain dealers and merchants of Bombay keeping the Island well supplied. The object contemplated in the draft letter to the Collector of North Canara was not to interfere with the market, but to be prepared against any extraordinary influx of strangers, whose support, of course, cannot have entered into the calculations of our grain dealers. These, besides retaining some in Bombay, we must distribute in different parts of Salsette, in Caranja, the Island of Elephanta, and elsewhere. In these places the supply of grain is not abundant, and not sufficient for the extra population that may be thrown upon them ; hence my object was to provide rice for these strangers to be employed in cleaning or constructing tanks and wells, by paying them in grain instead of money in places where the latter could not provide the former.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“Whenever any of the tanks in Bombay may be dry, and there are two or three already nearly so, measures should be immediately adopted <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-147" data-page="147" data-folio="135" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n146_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 135">[p. 135]</button> to excavate them to as great a depth as possible. The season, though unfortunately one of drought, is particularly favourable to the enlargement of our reservoirs for water, and by carrying it on to the greatest practical extent we provide means to lessen the severity in any future similar calamity. I dread a scarcity of water more than a scarcity of grain.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">" The associations that may be formed should be voluntary on the part of individuals, and the Government should not appear to have any connection with them. Any other interference than that of employing individuals on public works we should carefully avoid, as it will slacken the zeal and humane efforts of the leading Natives of the different castes by misleading them into a belief that the Government are going beyond what it is possible they can accomplish in what, there is reason to apprehend, will prove so general a distress."</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">89. On the above the following observations were recorded by the Governor and President, the Honorable Mountstuart Elphinstone:—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">" Though all fear of famine is, I hope, removed by the late fall of rain, we may still have scarcity to apprehend, and it may, at all events, be as well to record our opinions on this subject while it is under discussion, that we may be prepared if our present hopes should prove fallacious.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">" I must confess, I think, there is a great deal of truth in Mr. Warden's anticipations regarding the bounty. I was led to propose the measure from the example of Madras (which we had just been informed by that Government was perfectly successful) rather than by any examination of the subject on general principles : were the necessity to occur again I would propose, in preference, that Government should guarantee a certain price to all importers of grain. This price should be sufficient to counterbalance all the inducements which are held out at present by the demand for freight to the employment of tonnage in the ordinary branches of commerce. In other words, it should yield a little more profit on each ton than could be gained by any other sort of freight at present obtainable. I see no objection to this plan, provided the period at which the guarantee is to cease be judiciously fixed.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">" It is precisely the plan adopted in armies with Brinjaries, and it would probably be as successful, and proportionately as little burdensome, when used on a larger scale.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">" I very much question the expediency of Government taking part in associations, or, in fact, doing any thing in the way of direct relief to the people beyond giving employment to the poorest classes. I would rather have their pay in money than in grain, as long as grain was to be procured <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-148" data-page="148" data-folio="136" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n147_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 136">[p. 136]</button> at all; and if it was absolutely necessary to issue grain it should be in quantities considerably less than a labourer would consume in time of plenty. If a full allowance of grain is given to each labourer, it enables him to live as usual at the expense of the rest of the poor, to whom the surplus would be left if Government labourers were put on short allowance.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“I would not advise any importation of grain by Government, unless Government could bring forward any means of transport not possessed by private merchants; otherwise the quantity of grain imported would not be increased, and the distribution would be taken from the hands of those whose interest is the surest guide into those of Government, which is much less likely to conduct it with advantage. If Government had any spare tonnage it should be employed in bringing grain from distant markets, unless it were perfectly certain that the near ones possessed resources which the private tonnage could not exhaust even by repeated voyages.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“In addition to the undertakings recommended by Mr. Warden, I would propose that if there is a great want of employment, either in the Northern Concan or the neighbouring parts of the Deccan, the construction of the new road by the Thull Ghaut should be immediately begun, a detachment of Pioneers being drawn from the Poona road to assist and superintend.”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">90. In a further minute recorded by Mr. Warden he wrote—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“I still question the policy of the plan suggested by the Governor, and am persuaded that a scarcity price constitutes a sufficient stimulus to the import of grain into Bombay from those countries whence we are ordinarily supplied. The tender from Remington Crawford, and the larger or equal quantity of grain to which Mr. Goodwin alludes as intended for Bombay, prove the measures which the merchants timely pursue to benefit by the high price of grain without the necessity for any encouragement on the part of the Government. In these two instances Rs. 24,000 would have been paid in rice which was destined for Bombay weeks before any bounty was thought of. The tonnage ordinarily employed between Bombay and Calcutta is now engaged as transports at an exorbitant rate of freight, on terms infinitely more profitable than we could possibly hold out for their employment in bringing rice. Had we been reduced to the severity of famine but one alternative would have remained, to have released some of the transports and allowed them to have supplied us with rice.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“If a famine were apprehended in Bengal and in those countries whence we draw our supplies of grain, we should be reduced to the necessity of looking to a distant quarter for relief—to the Cape for instance, or even to Europe—as the United Kingdom looked to India for supplies of rice in 1800, and encouraged its import into Great Britain. In such a <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-149" data-page="149" data-folio="137" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n148_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 137">[p. 137]</button> contingency the policy of the measure suggested by the Governor is undoubted, but so long as one part of India is able to supply another I feel confident that no inducement the Government can hold out will encourage a greater supply than would have been thrown in by the urgency of the demand and the great profit accruing from high prices."</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">91. The result of this discussion was the abandonment of the proposition to offer a bounty to importers of grain, as stated above. Government, however, in the mean time, on the representation of the Collectors of Broach and Surat, authorized the Magistrates to forbid the exportation of grain to foreign countries where a similar prohibition existed regarding exportation to the British Territories; this prohibition in respect to the Guikwar being deferred till His Highness had had an opportunity of communicating, through the Resident, his views and intentions regarding the freedom of the grain trade.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">92. Measures were also directed to be taken for digging wells wherever it could be done with advantage in Bombay; a Committee was appointed by Government accordingly, consisting of the Collector of Bombay, the Chief Engineer, the Revenue Surveyor, and the Secretary to the Medical Board. This measure was thought of primary importance, as apprehensions of a failure of water in Bombay were also beginning to be entertained. The average yearly fall of rain in the Island of Bombay was about 84 inches. But in 1824 by the end of August only 25 inches had fallen. The Committee were, therefore, directed to take into their careful consideration the subject of procuring a sufficient supply of water, and the Revenue Surveyor, who was also a Member of the Committee, was empowered to give effect to the Committee's suggestions.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">93. From the reports submitted to Government from time to time regarding the state of the different Collectorates, the degree of scarcity felt or apprehended in them, and the supplies of grain in hand or expected, it appears that the monsoon proved more or less unfavorable in almost every part of the Bombay Presidency. In the early part of the season rain fell as usual in some of the districts, but there were long continued droughts, broken only by slight and desultory showers, which only encouraged delusive <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-150" data-page="150" data-folio="138" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n149_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 138">[p. 138]</button> hopes. Thus the crops withered away in places where there was no irrigation, and only yielded a scanty harvest in places where means of irrigation existed. In other districts the rain was so late that the cultivation of early crops could not be undertaken, and even the later crops suffered to some extent. The consequence was that the produce of the year was quite insufficient to meet the wants of the people. Fortunately, however, the harvests of the preceding two years had been abundant, and there was thus a considerable surplus in hand in several parts of Goozerat and Kattyawar. The effects of the scarcity, which would otherwise have been severely felt, were thus rendered less calamitous generally, though the sufferings of the poorer classes of the population were, perhaps, as great as during any of the previous famines.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">94. Numbers of people appear to have emigrated to Bombay in quest of food, and instructions were given to all the Collectors and other Officers to avail themselves, as much as possible, of the presence of these individuals for the construction of public works, of which many were then started with a view expressly to afford relief to the new comers.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">95. The year 1825, which succeeded the above period, also proved to be unfavorable in several parts of the Presidency. The rain was almost everywhere below the average. The produce of the year was thus very limited, scarcely enough to meet the year's consumption. The stock in hand from the produce of the preceding year was small owing to the long continued drought which prevailed during that year, and, as a natural result from this state of things, the price of grain ranged high, so high at times as to occasion considerable distress to the poorer classes, and, as in the preceding year, it became necessary for Government to remit the import duties. Public works were also started at the expense of Government with a view to afford relief to the most distressed of the Ryots.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">96. It appears that, owing to the scarcity which prevailed during the preceding (1824) year, Government had appointed a Committee, denominated the "Committee of Tanks and Wells," in <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-151" data-page="151" data-folio="139" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n150_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 139">[p. 139]</button> Bombay, consisting of the Land Revenue Collector, the Chief Engineer, and the Secretary to the Medical Board. This Committee had for its object, as implied by its designation, the improvement and increase of the water resources in the Town and Island of Bombay. The Committee were allowed the services of an Engineer to frame plans and estimates of such works as to them might seem necessary with this view. Under their superintendence several wells were repaired and improved, and new ones sunk in localities in which additional water supply was most wanted. At considerable expense to Government many of the large tanks both in Bombay and Mahim were deepened and widened so as to contain a greater storage. The result of their operations is thus recorded by the Committee—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“We have the satisfaction to observe that none of the measures can be said to have altogether failed that were at our suggestions adopted, while the advantages have been, and are likely to be, great from many of them ; the resources still seemingly in reserve along the western side of the Esplanade being such as to justify the expectation that the wants of the present season will, by means of temporary wells, to be increased in number as occasion may require in that direction, in the Fort Ditch and in Dhoby’s Tank, be yet in an adequate degree provided for.”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">97. In the Southern Concan, owing to the deficiency of rain during the monsoon of this year, the crops on the salt lands were entirely destroyed, which occasioned considerable distress among the cultivating classes. As a consequence of this disaster the district authorities are stated to have been literally inundated with applications from the poorer Ryots for advances of money to enable them to purchase seed and to procure the means of subsistence. These applications, it is said, could not be complied with owing to the difficulty of determining the cases of real distress ; yet every possible assistance was afforded by the Collector and his Assistants to relieve the poorer classes by employing them on works of public utility. The road leading from the Ghauts to the Salt Bunder, on which the traffic was great during the fair season, but which in many places was steep and rugged, was considerably improved. Wells and tanks were also constructed. These works, while they afforded to a certain extent relief to the needy poor, were of great general benefit to the country.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-152"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="152" data-folio="140" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n151_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 140">[p. 140]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">98. In the Northern Concan, though no distress had yet been felt to any perceptible degree, yet serious apprehensions were entertained of a very unfavourable state of things in that respect. In order to avert or modify the effects of any future calamity which sooner or later might befall the Ryots, the Collector submitted a proposition for cleaning the wells and tanks in the several parts of the district under his supervision, as it was thought that, while a timely supply of water could thus be secured, means would be afforded to the needy of supporting themselves to some extent. The cleaning of tanks and wells was urgently pressed upon the notice of Government by the Collectors in consequence of the scanty supply of water caused by deficient rain in the preceding monsoon. The cost of these works, however, as estimated appeared to Government to be excessive; but in consideration of the urgency of the occasion, as put forth by the Collector, he was authorized to commence upon such of the works proposed as were absolutely and urgently required. A number of tanks and wells were accordingly cleaned at a cost of about ten thousand Rupees.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">99. It will be remembered that during the preceding year, owing to the scarcity of food, a large number of people had migrated from Kutch to Bombay. Upwards of a thousand souls had come to Bombay alone under the leadership of one Suwayee Raja, who, on arrival, petitioned Government on their behalf with a view to procure them employment. This application was readily granted, and the Collector of Tanna and H. M’s. Justices of the Peace were requested to arrange accordingly. They were to be paid at such rate of remuneration as was earned by a common day labourer. Measures were also directed to be taken to shelter these unfortunates, and to afford them other requisite aid. Under requisition from the Collector of Tanna, some of these emigrants were placed at the disposal of that Officer to be employed on the construction of a new road proposed by him. The remainder were placed at the disposal of General Bellasis to be employed on works then in course of construction under his superintendence. After a few months, however, on hearing of an improved state of things in their own country (Kutch), they expressed a wish to be <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-153" data-page="153" data-folio="141" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n152_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 141">[p. 141]</button> allowed to return. It was at first a question whether they should be sent back at the expense of Government. It was decided that they should be, and they were accordingly provided with a passage at the public expense. The proceedings on record connected with the scarcity of grain during the year, and the measures adopted to alleviate the distress of the people, do not show exactly what works of a permanent nature and of public utility were projected or completed. Indeed, it would appear from the information available on the subject that nothing beyond wells and tanks was thought of. However, the works, though of minor importance, went a great way towards affording the much required relief in places where they were constructed.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">100. In 1833 again considerable distress seems to have prevailed in most of the districts under the Bombay Presidency, including almost all the Collectorates above the Ghauts, as also Tanna, Rutnagherry, Surat, Kaira, and Ahmedabad.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">101. In Ahmednuggur the Collector, in writing upon the distress prevalent in his districts occasioned by the high price of food and other necessaries of life, observed as follows:—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“A remission in some sort, at least of the transit duties on grain, should, I conceive, be adopted without further delay; the prices are already so high as to occasion very severe distress; the fate of the Rubbee crop seems sealed by the entire want of rain hitherto, and though the bringing in of the Khureef crops has to a certain extent lowered the prices in Nuggur within these few days, yet I have little idea that the relief will be more than temporary. Without some extraneous supply, the prices must, I should fear, rise for several months, and not only are the present prices so high as to be ruinous to the poor, but the quality of grain generally exposed for sale is so bad as, I fear, to be likely to entail disease on those who have no other food.”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">102. The Collector was of opinion that the remission proposed by him should be general on all grain imported in any part of his Collectorate from whatever direction, and likewise on the grain removed from one part of his district to another, or to any part of the British territory beyond his jurisdiction. The Revenue Commissioner, however, did not quite concur with the Collector in the necessity for sanctioning a general remission of duties <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-154" data-page="154" data-folio="142" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n153_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 142">[p. 142]</button> throughout the Collectorate. He authorized the Collector to adopt his recommendation in such Talookas, or parts of Talookas, as suffered most, and where a remission would really be productive of beneficial results. In the town of Ahmednuggur the sufferings of the people were so far aggravated by the steady rise in prices that the duties levied were at once remitted under this sanction, and the remission continued in force so long as the distress lasted.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">103. In reporting his proceedings to Government the Revenue Commissioner observed regarding the remission authorized by him “that the benefits of this measure may be expected to be two-fold. In the first place the remission ought to have a direct effect in reducing prices, &amp;c.; and in the second it may be expected to aid towards breaking the combination of the grain dealers by establishing a mart under regulations as it were whose effect it will be difficult for them to counteract.” How far these anticipations were realized there is no record to show, but it appears that there was a favorable turn of things towards the end of the year. There was an abundant Khureef harvest, and the duties on grain which had been temporarily taken off were again imposed.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">104. The following extract is descriptive of the state of the Dharwar Collecterate as regards the prospects of the season:—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“The state of the crops is variable; some Talookas to the east, for instance Indee and Moodbibihal, have suffered immensely. Taking the whole Collectorate into consideration, it may be said that only about half the usual produce will be collected.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“The Rubbee crops have greatly failed, and disappointed the different hopes that were entertained.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“In respect to the grain in store, the general opinion is that, with the produce of the present year, there is sufficient for the year’s consumption, though, from the combination among the grain dealers, distress will be felt to a very great extent.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“From what has occurred in Dharwar it appears that, though grain is comparatively abundant, yet the dealers refuse to dispose of it in sufficient quantities, and had thereby, in fact, commenced to create a state of distress which the real state of things did not warrant. I have in some measure for the present counteracted this by informing the dealers that if they refused to sell (they fixing their own price) that I should grant free passes to any <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-155" data-page="155" data-folio="143" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n154_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 143">[p. 143]</button> individuals to bring in grain. This had the desired effect, and I have hope that difficulty from future combination will be avoided.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"Hitherto I have remitted the duties only in Dharwar and the Talookas of Indee and Moodibihal, but it has been thought desirable, especially by Mr. Elliot, the Sub-Collector of Hooblee, whose opinions are entitled to the greatest weight, that they should be remitted throughout, in order that the benefit may be distributed equally, and be felt by the whole community.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"I have, however, still an opinion that the plan of granting free passes for grain brought to different Towns and Talookas where called for is a good one, as tending to a more direct assistance to the most distressed spots, and creating an immediate supply where most called for. It defeats combination, it facilitates adjustment with the different farmers, and I think it will be generally found effective in its end.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>"There is a considerable quantity of grain in the hands of the more wealthy Ryots, who in fact will realize considerable profits in this year of scarcity: it is this grain purchased from them, or carried by themselves to the distressed spots, that would create the great relief under the system of passes. It would be the object, as a matter of speculation, to ascertain where the grain was most required, and there it would be immediately taken. The passes should be granted by the Collector, the Sub-Collector, and Assistants in charge of Talookas, so that no abuse might be occasioned from favor; and being generally known throughout the districts, I have no question they would be eagerly sought."</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">105. In consideration of the circumstances described in the foregoing extract, the Collector was authorized by Government at his discretion either to remit the duties on grain or to introduce the pass system, as recommended by him. In the event of his adopting the first of the two alternatives suggested for encouraging the importation of grain from places where there might be abundance of supply, the Collector was vested with power to make such arrangements as to him seemed best with a view to compensate the farmer of the duties for any loss <em>bonâ fide</em> incurred by him.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">106. In Rutnagerce there was supposed to be enough grain to meet the year's consumption, but much eagerness was manifested by the grain dealers to export their surplus stock. Consequently the prices ranged so high as to operate severely upon the poorer classes. No interference, however, of any kind was rendered <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-156" data-page="156" data-folio="144" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n155_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 144">[p. 144]</button> necessary on the part of Government. The effect of this rise in prices, though unwelcome to the great body of consumers and cultivators, was beneficial to the public revenue. From the statement of the sale of "revenue grain" for the year, it appears that there was an increase of Rs. 73,693 over the revenue of the preceding year. This increase was, of course, attributable to the enhanced prices, and to the eagerness of the speculators to secure as much grain as possible for exportation to places where they hoped to reap the largest profits.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">107. At Sholapoor the duties on grain had been remitted under the sanction of Government for some time; but, notwithstanding this, the high prices remained unaffected. A proposal was, therefore, submitted for the consideration of Government to sanction the construction of certain public works with a view to afford temporary relief. Government, however, did not consider the distress prevailing at Sholapoor to be so severe as to justify the adoption of the proposition made by the Sub-Collector.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">108. In Tanna, owing to the badness of the season, considerable defalcation in the produce of the year was apprehended; yet from the stock in hand, which was estimated to suffice for the year's consumption, the distress arising from enhanced prices was comparatively less severe than in most other places.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">109. The scarcity which prevailed in Poona and the districts under it invited large importations of grain from the Nizam's dominions, as also from Sattara and other places. The effect of these importations was to keep the prices steady, though it did not lower them; but it was apprehended that so soon as the season should somewhat advance, and the facilities for carrying grain should become less from the great difficulty to be expected in procuring forage and water for the cattle, prices of all sorts would very materially rise, and that, though there was little ground to fear an actual famine, sooner or later severe distress would overtake the poorer classes of the community. The Collector, therefore, recommended a general remission of the transit duties on the grain imported to any part of his Collectorate. He was of opinion that a remission of this duty might induce merchants in <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-157" data-page="157" data-folio="145" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n156_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 145">[p. 145]</button> the Nizam's dominions and other grain producing countries to export grain to Poona, and would contribute more than any other measure to lessen the severity of the distress. With a view also to encourage the importation of grain from Bombay and Goozerat at the same time, the Collector likewise suggested a reduction in the duties collected by the Sea Customs Department at the various Bunders, and recommended the suspension of the toll at the Bhore Ghaut, which, he remarked, had had the effect of changing the route by which the carriers of the country travelled. This toll, the Collector observed, did not realize very much, and its remission or suspension would be a real saving to Government.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">110. In handing this letter on to Government the Revenue Commissioner observed—"I do not think the duties leviable on the routes impose any material restriction on the passage of grain; and on that imported by sea from Goozerat and other countries, whose surplus stock enable them to send large supplies to the Deccan, no transit duty whatever is charged, the certificate of payment of sea customs exempting them from inland duties, &amp;c.; and that, therefore, any slight alleviating measure, like the suspension of road duties, would only swell the profits of the grain dealers without bringing any material relief to the consumers." With reference to the duties collected by the Sea Customs Department, the Revenue Commissioner was of opinion that there was no sufficient reason for lowering them, as exportation of grain from Bombay and other sea port towns was influenced not so much by the levy of sea customs as by the actual state of the market, or, in other words, by the state of prices. The Collector's proposal to suspend the transit duty on grain and to reduce the sea customs levy was accordingly negatived by Government. They, however, requested the Revenue Commissioner to suspend the Bhore Ghaut toll, if he agreed with the Collector, the suspension being confined to bullocks, carts, &amp;c., conveying grain to the upper country. It was thought probable that such a measure would tend to encourage the transport of grain direct from the Concan.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">111. In Surat a very serious falling off in the produce of the year was at first apprehended, and a consequent rise in the prices of the principal articles of food caused much distress. Towards <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-158" data-page="158" data-folio="146" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n157_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 146">[p. 146]</button> the end of the season, however, there was a good fall of rain, and the later crops prospered well, yielding sufficient to meet the wants of the people for a year. But, unfortunately for the poor, the merchants were bent upon benefiting themselves from the enhanced prices obtaining in other districts, and commenced a general exportation of their stock. The result, as might be expected, was that, instead of falling off, the prices steadily continued to rise, till indeed they almost reached famine rates. A petition, numerously signed by the inhabitants, was then presented to the Assistant Judge, requesting the good offices of that gentleman to induce Government to prohibit the exportation of grain from their city. This petition was, however, negatived by Government. The Assistant Judge in transmitting the petition remarked upon the desirability of remitting the transit duties on grain, and of suspending the sea customs both on imports and exports, but, after consulting the Revenue Commissioner and the chief Customs authority on the subject, Government did not think that such measures were called for by the exigencies of the case.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">112. Regarding the distress which prevailed at Belgaum, the Brigadier General Commanding that Division of the Army wrote as follows:—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“It is with extreme regret I have to report, for the information of His Excellency the Commander-in-Chief, the great want of rain in this part of the country.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“The consequences of it are that the prices of all the necessaries of life have risen enormously; that the greatest difficulties exist in getting supplies of grain sufficient for the European and Native troops and followers; that for some days numbers, both of sepoys and followers, were unable to procure any; and that at this moment the quantity, both in the Camp Bazar and Town of Belgaum, is so small that unless fresh supplies are brought in we shall be in great distress. I have specially to report that, owing to the scarcity, the Bread Contractor to His Majesty’s 20th Regiment has failed, he being unable not only to fulfil his contract on account of the immense rise on corn, but unable to procure a sufficient quantity of money.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“The price of bread has risen within these few months from 16 seers in April to 7 seers, the present price, in August, and I have been compelled in consequence, agreeably to Article 109, Section 50, of the Supplement Military Code, to transfer to the Commissariat the provisioning of the European Troops.</p></blockquote></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-159"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="159" data-folio="147" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n158_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 147">[p. 147]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>" Under these distressing circumstances, and the present very unfavorable appearance of the weather (not a tank or well having any increase of water since May last), I respectfully suggest that His Excellency the Commander-in-Chief will be pleased to submit this application to His Lordship in Council for a remission of the duties on all supplies coming to this camp, that every facility and encouragement be given to induce dealers to bring in grain and cattle, agreeably to Section 8, Article 69, Second Supplement Military Code, Bazar Regulations.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>" No jowaree, no grain, no wheat, is procurable here at any price, nor have they been for some days."</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">113. In another communication upon the same subject, after describing the effects of the scarcity prevalent in Belgaum, the Brigadier General stated—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>" Under these circumstances I am induced to bring to the notice of His Excellency the Commander-in-Chief, with a view to His Excellency's support in laying the matter before Government, the great advantage that might be derived by employing a hundred or so of the starving population upon the road from this station towards the Ram Ghaut under the orders of the Assistant Quarter Master General.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>" This road from hence to the Ghaut has been untouched for some years, and is very much out of repair in many places; so much so as to render it a matter of great difficulty and hinderance in bringing up heavy ordnance stores and arrack from the coast, and in the present state of distress (which, I regret to state, exists to an alarming extent) the temporary employment of many of these poor creatures may be attended with great advantage to the Government, and perhaps be the means of preventing their resorting to unlawful acts for a livelihood, and disturbing the tranquillity of the country.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>" The prices of grain are not only extremely high, but the quantity offered for sale is barely adequate to the demand, and from present appearances the general dread of famine is so great that I deem it my duty to request you to make known these my sentiments to His Excellency, and to suggest that, as a temporary measure, if <em>the duties on grain coming into camp for sale were taken off</em> it would tend to lower the price considerably."</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">114. In laying these communications before Government His Excellency the Commander-in-Chief recommended compliance with the Brigadier General's proposals, and Government were accordingly pleased to suspend the duties on grain in Belgaum, and at the same time to sanction the repairs to the Ram Ghaut road, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-160" data-page="160" data-folio="148" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n159_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 148">[p. 148]</button> with a view to giving employment to those destitute persons who might be willing and able to work.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">115. The scarcity which prevailed at Ahmedabad and Kaira is stated to have occasioned considerable distress to the inhabitants, to alleviate which Government allowed the transit duties on grain to be remitted until better prospects opened. The sea customs duty was also reduced on the recommendation of the Customs Collector for Goozerat.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">116. In 1838 considerable distress appears to have prevailed in the provinces of Kutch and Kattyawar owing to scarcity of food. The records of the year, however, connected with the subject do not show exactly what measures were adopted by the Native Governments to alleviate the distress. But the scarcity must have been great, if it be judged of from the number of the inhabitants who, with their entire families, left those provinces in search of the means of subsistence where food was procurable. Upwards of two thousand souls are said to have taken refuge in Bombay and in other places under this Presidency.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">117. The fall of rain in the Island of Bombay was much less than usual; so small indeed that most of the principal wells and tanks were not even half filled with water. This scarcity of water, coupled with the circumstance of a large number of emigrants from Kattyawar having flocked to Bombay, filled the minds of the Natives with serious apprehensions. Some of the leading members of the community accordingly addressed a petition to Government, representing the real state of things, and exhorting Government to take prompt action in the matter and provide against the impending calamity.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">118. This was, perhaps, the first intimation that Government received of apprehensions of scarcity of water in Bombay, and of the arrival of such large numbers of emigrants from the famine-stricken districts. No time was lost in taking the necessary steps to allay the misgivings.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">119. Acting in conformity with the proceedings adopted during the scarcity of 1824, Government appointed a Committee in <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-161" data-page="161" data-folio="149" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n160_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 149">[p. 149]</button> Bombay, composed of the Collector, the Chief Engineer, the Secretary to the Medical Board, and the Superintendent of Repairs. To these were subsequently added the names of three Native gentlemen, Messrs. Framjee Cowasjee, Dhackjee Dadajee, and Sir (then Mr.) Jamsetjee Jejeebhoy. The Committee were charged with the duty of enquiring into and reporting the real extent of the scarcity of water on the island, as also the number of sufferers from famine who were stated to have resorted to Bombay in search of food from Kattyawar and other places. The Committee was also desired to recommend such measures as appeared to them best calculated to provide effectually against the apprehended calamity.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">120. The attention of the Superintendent of Roads was at the same time directed to the arrivals from Kattyawar and Kutch, with a view to arrangements being made to provide them with employment on the works then under construction under his orders.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">121. In accordance with the suggestions of the Committee, the several district authorities were instructed to furnish weekly returns of the prices of grains in their respective districts for publication. The Committee were of opinion that the publication of such price current would be a very useful measure, since wherever prices were unusually high a knowledge of the fact would operate, it was hoped, as a sufficient incentive with the grain merchants to export their grain to such places. This would do away with the necessity of offering any thing in the shape of bounty, which appears to have been recommended on the present occasion, and was even granted in one or two previous instances of scarcity of grain. The utmost freedom of trade was also enjoined, and periodical reports were ordered to be submitted to Government of the degree of distress prevailing in the several districts, with such recommendations as to the measures for affording aid to the sufferers as the Collectors might think proper to make.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">122. At the same time a proclamation was issued by Government in the Town and Island of Bombay, prohibiting people from bathing or washing their clothes, &amp;c., in any of the public fresh water wells and tanks, and from otherwise wasting the water. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-162" data-page="162" data-folio="150" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n161_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 150">[p. 150]</button> In order that this prohibition might be effectually carried out, all such wells and tanks were placed under the orders of the Superintendent of Police, who was directed to make the necessary arrangements for their preservation and protection. Watering the roads with fresh water was interdicted, and strict orders were issued against washing and watering cattle elsewhere than in the Fort Ditch, which was made convenient for the purpose. Some very important alterations appear to have been sanctioned by Government to the Washerman's Tank.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">123.
