Following, perhaps, the example in Bengal on the occasion of a famine, the public of Bombay appointed a Committee for the relief of the sufferers in Bombay and elsewhere. The exact circumstances connected with this movement are not, however, on record, nor does it appear what measures were adopted for the collection of the requisite funds for affording aid to the sufferers. But frequent mention is made of large sums of money having been placed by the Relief Fund Committee at the disposal of the authorities concerned for the maintenance of the old and decrepit, the sick and the infirm. A large number of the able bodied of these sufferers was at the same time placed under the Superintendent of Roads, to be employed on the Panwell and Oorun line, on such wages as appeared to be sufficient for their maintenance. The out-turn of work by these emigrants was, however, found to be far below that performed by ordinary labourers, and the consequence was that Government had to expend more than the estimate wherever works were constructed by emigrant labour. In consideration of this circumstance, and the very great expense attendant upon the maintenance of these emigrants, the Superintendent of Roads, after intimation had been received of a good fall of rain in Kattyawar, suggested the advisability of returning them to their country. The Famine Relief Committee consented, and further agreed to bear the expense of providing them with a passage back. But the emigrants refused to go, and it was with difficulty that they could be prevailed upon to proceed to the Deccan during the monsoon, when the work on the Panwell Road was stopped. Government therefore, on the suggestion of the Superintendent of Roads, directed that, as there was no longer any necessity for <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-163" data-page="163" data-folio="151" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n162_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 151">[p. 151]</button> the State to provide the emigrants with employment, they should be left to find employment for themselves as other biggaries, or that they should be put on smaller wages, which might induce them to return to their country. There is nothing on the records to show how far these orders were carried out, and with what result. The monthly expense of maintaining these emigrants averaged between 1,500 and 2,000. The proportion charged to the Famine Fund is not distinctly stated, but it appears that the whole expense, and feeding, and otherwise maintaining, the old and the sick who accompanied the other emigrants, was borne by the Committee.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">124. The Managers of the Bombay Pinjrapole, a charitable Institution established through the liberality of some Native gentlemen of Bombay, also extended their support to the refugees. Finding that numbers of them were without any shelter, the Managers offered to provide them with accommodation on the premises of the Pinjrapole until they could be provided with suitable employment, and even undertook to share the expense of their maintenance with the Famine Fund Committee. Also, during the rainy season, when the works on which the emigrants were employed were stopped, the Managers of the Pinjrapole consented to take charge of them, and attend to their wants and comforts.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">125. Besides the Panwell and Oorun Road, the Panwell Pier appears to have been constructed about this time by emigrant labour. A new road from Caranja, communicating with the main line at Konee, was likewise sanctioned by Government about this time, and a large party of emigrants was employed upon it. A road from Panwell to Hog Island was also projected during the same year, and plans and estimates of the expense of construction were submitted to Government. The road between Dharwar and Belgaum was, too, at this time completed, but no emigrants appear to have been employed on this work.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">126. A new line of road was proposed to supersede that leading from Belgaum over the Ram Ghaut, but the scheme was abandoned after considerable discussion. A project was then formed and sanctioned for making improvements to the existing road. Government on this occasion, at the suggestion of the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-164" data-page="164" data-folio="152" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n163_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 152">[p. 152]</button> Executive Engineer in charge of the districts, allowed the use of a Company of Sappers and Miners for the purpose.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">127. Considerable distress appears to have prevailed about the same time in the Hunsote Pergunnah of the Surat Collectorate. The Collector reported that numbers of people were undergoing all the horrors of starvation owing to the total failure of crops in about thirty villages of that Pergunnah, and he, therefore, recommended the grant of an advance of about 13,000 Rupees, to enable them to purchase food and seed for the ensuing season. The Collector added that if this aid was denied Government would be losers to the extent of many times that amount, as most of the cultivators in that case would be compelled to leave their homes and lands, which would thus be left uncultivated. The urgency of the occasion left no alternative to Government but to sanction the grant applied for.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">128. There are no records describing the state of the other Collectorates, except Belgaum, Dharwar and Ahmedabad. In these Collectorates the prospects of the season were not so favorable as might be wished. Yet no distress seems to have been felt in any of them to the extent of necessitating Government action.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">129. In 1845 great distress arose in Candeish. It lasted only a short time, but such were its effects that fears were entertained regarding the preservation of the public peace. This distress was the result for the most part of a combination among the grain dealers, who would not bring sufficient grain into the market though there was no scarcity of it, their object being to reap as large profits as possible at the expense of the poorer classes. The falling off in the cultivation of the edible grain in Malwa and other places which generally supplied Candeish afforded the dealers a pretext for raising the prices, and not exposing sufficient quantities of grain for sale. The Regiments stationed at Malligaum suffered greatly from the difficulty of procuring the requisite supplies of food even at the highest prices which then ruled in Candeish. The Collector used every endeavour to mitigate the evil, without interfering with the proceedings of the grain dealers, or restricting the freedom of the market, but unsuccessfully. He accordingly <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-165" data-page="165" data-folio="153" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n164_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 153">[p. 153]</button> reported the matter to Government, proposing certain measures for the relief of the sufferers; among which he suggested the advisability of Government taking steps to obtain grain from one of the neighbouring provinces where there might be an abundance. The adoption, or even the rumour, of such a measure, he thought, would have the effect of opening the granaries and bringing forth the hidden stock at once.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">130. On receipt of the Collector's report Government directed copies of it to be forwarded to the Military Board and to the Resident at Indore, who were requested to be good enough to give Government the benefit of their views as to the best means of obtaining the requisite supplies. In reply, the Resident stated that there was abundance of grain in Bhopal, and that he would arrange to send as much as might be required. Meanwhile, however, the high prices which prevailed in Candeish attracted the attention of foreign grain merchants, and a considerable quantity of grain was imported into the locality. This not only relieved the wants of the people, but it tended to break the combination of the grain sellers, and to bring down prices to the means of consumers. There was thus no occasion for Government to import grain on its own account for the use of the Regiments stationed at Malligaum.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">131. In order that Government might be in possession of full and reliable information regarding the state of grain markets throughout the Mofussil, the two Revenue Commissioners were directed to submit returns within stated intervals showing the state of prices of grains in the districts under their respective charge, together with the quantity of each kind supposed to be available for sale. These returns appear to have been regularly made until the opening of the new harvest.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">132. Owing to a scanty fall of rain in the early part of the season of 1862, a scarcity of food prevailed to a considerable extent in the districts of Poona, Ahmednuggur, Sholapoor, Candeish, and part of Sattara. So great was the scarcity that a famine was considered inevitable unless prompt measures were adopted.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">133. An account of the sufferings occasioned by the scarcity which prevailed in the districts above mentioned, and of the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-166" data-page="166" data-folio="154" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n165_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 154">[p. 154]</button> measures adopted by Government on the occasion, will be found in the accompanying printed correspondence originating in a letter from Mr. Hart, then Revenue Commissioner S. D., representing the inconvenience and hardship suffered by the low-paid servants of Government. As a most effectual mode of affording the necessary relief, numerous works of public utility were sanctioned by Government on which unskilled labour might be usefully employed. A statement of the works which were authorized to be undertaken for this purpose is given in the correspondence above referred to.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">134. Several other works were also sanctioned by Government on the representation of the local authorities. A list of these works is appended:—</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>List of Works sanctioned in addition to the Famine Relief Works.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><ul class="editorial-list"><li>1. A road from Challisgaum to Dhoolia.</li><li>2. Do. Munmar to Malligaum.</li><li>3. Do. Nuggur to Kurmalla.</li><li>4. Do. Patus to Dhond.</li><li>5. Do. Serroor to Khairgaum.</li><li>6. Improvement of the road from Nuggur to Serroor.</li><li>7. Chundapoor Ghaut.</li><li>8. A road from Kurmalla to Jehoor.</li><li>9. Do. Punderpoor to Mahole (not commenced).</li><li>10. Do. Sholapoor to Beejapoor.</li><li>11. Do. Tembhoornee to Purunda <em>viâ</em> Keion.</li><li>12. Do. Nargaum to Burhanpoor.</li><li>13. Do. Taptee to Bhoreghur.</li><li>14. Repairs to Gowtalla and Purdhunee Ghaut.</li><li>15. A road from Ahmednuggur to Balaghaut.</li><li>16. Poona and Nassick road.</li><li>17. Kolhar and Nandoor road.</li><li>18. A road from Pyton to bottom of Imampoor.</li><li>19. Do. Loonud to Punderpoor.</li><li>20. Improving road from Beejapoor to Kurrar.</li></ul></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">135. For the relief of the Government servants drawing salaries of less than Rs. 200 per mensem, who were placed in a <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-167" data-page="167" data-folio="155" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n166_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 155">[p. 155]</button> most unfortunate position by the most unprecedented rise in the prices of all articles of food, &amp;c., Government sanctioned temporarily the grant of grain compensation according to a fixed scale. The correspondence connected with this subject is appended.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">136. In 1864 some distress was felt in Candeish, rendering it necessary for Government to afford their aid in relieving the wants of the people, and, agreeably to the suggestions of the Revenue Commissioner N. D., Government sanctioned the adoption of the following measures :—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><ul class="editorial-list"><li>I—To proceed with the Dhoolia and Challisgaum road.</li><li>II—The construction of Sailabaree road, near Pimpulnair.</li><li>III—The employment of the Bheels on the Northern Frontiers on a portion of the road to join Seerpoor and Torkhedah.</li></ul></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">137. Considerable distress was reported to prevail at Rajcote, and Government sanctioned an outlay of Rs. 15,000 for constructing the road from Baumunbore to Choteyla.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">138. In 1865 the Collector of Surat brought to notice a very distressing state of affairs in some parts of his districts, and recommended the grant of Rs. 5,000 a month for three months for tank excavation. In consideration of the urgency of the occasion, the application was conceded by Government.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">139. A similar grant was made in the Kaira Collectorate for the construction and improvement of tanks and wells with a view to afford relief to the poorer classes suffering from the scarcity of food and from the general high prices of provisions.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">140. In August 1865 the Resident at Aden reported the prevalence of very severe distress among the poorer classes of people at that Port. Every possible arrangement was made by him to find employment for the people in the Public Works Department. There were, however, numbers of people without employ, who were stated to be in a starving condition. He, therefore, projected certain other works with a view to affording temporary relief to the sufferers. These works were approved of <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-168" data-page="168" data-folio="156" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/cu31924091209449/page/n167_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 156">[p. 156]</button> by Government, and an expenditure of Rs. 2,000 was sanctioned for the purpose, as recommended by the Resident. This, however, appeared to be insufficient, and on the urgent representation of the Resident a further sum of Rs. 4,800 was placed at the disposal of that Officer for the construction of certain retaining walls at Aden.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">141. This brief Summary contains all the information forthcoming from the records of the Secretariat.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have the honor to be,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Sir,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Your most obedient Servant,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">A. T. ETHERIDGE, Alienation Settlement Officer S. D.</p></div><section class="apparatus-zone" aria-label="Footnotes"><h2 class="apparatus-heading">Critical Apparatus &amp; Notes</h2><ol class="apparatus-list"><li id="sec-03-fn-001" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><strong class="fn-backlink fn-unlinked">[Unlinked note, p. 57]</strong> <span class="fn-body">Compiled from Monier Williams' Memoir of Broach Zillah.</span></li><li id="sec-03-fn-002" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-03-fn-002" aria-label="Return to text">*.</a> <span class="fn-body">“<em>Note</em>—Inside the walls of the old town of Sooltanpoor, now inhabited by three families of Coonbees and some Bheels, the old Patel four days ago pointed out to me 200 <em>wells</em>, which he recollects having been all in use in 1802.”</span></li><li id="sec-03-fn-003" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-03-fn-003" aria-label="Return to text">*.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>i. e.</em> the seer of 140 Tolas.</span></li></ol></section>
